# Self-Improvement in Language Models: The Sharpening Mechanism

Audrey Huang [audreyh5@illinois.edu](mailto:audreyh5@illinois.edu) Adam
Block¹¹footnotemark: 1
[blockadam@microsoft.com](mailto:blockadam@microsoft.com) Dylan J.
Foster¹¹footnotemark: 1
[dylanfoster@microsoft.com](mailto:dylanfoster@microsoft.com) Dhruv
Rohatgi [drohatgi@mit.edu](mailto:drohatgi@mit.edu) and Cyril Zhang
[cyrilzhang@microsoft.com](mailto:cyrilzhang@microsoft.com) Max
Simchowitz [msimchow@andrew.cmu.edu](mailto:msimchow@andrew.cmu.edu)
Jordan T. Ash
[ash.jordan@microsoft.com](mailto:ash.jordan@microsoft.com) Akshay
Krishnamurthy [akshaykr@microsoft.com](mailto:akshaykr@microsoft.com)
^(†)^(†)thanks: Equal contribution.

###### Abstract

Recent work in language modeling has raised the possibility of
_self-improvement_, where a language models evaluates and refines its
own generations to achieve higher performance without external feedback.
It is impossible for this self-improvement to create information that is
not already in the model, so why should we expect that this will lead to
improved capabilities?

We offer a new perspective on the capabilities of self-improvement
through a lens we refer to as _sharpening_. Motivated by the observation
that language models are often better at verifying response quality than
they are at generating correct responses, we formalize self-improvement
as using the model itself as a verifier during post-training in order to
“sharpen” the model to one placing large mass on high-quality sequences,
thereby amortizing the expensive inference-time computation of
generating good sequences. We begin by introducing a new statistical
framework for sharpening in which the learner aims to sharpen a
pre-trained base policy via sample access, and establish fundamental
limits. Then, we analyze two natural families of self-improvement
algorithms based on SFT and RLHF. We find that (i) the SFT-based
approach is minimax optimal whenever the initial model has sufficient
coverage, but (ii) the RLHF-based approach can improve over SFT-based
self-improvement by leveraging online exploration, bypassing the need
for coverage. Finally, we empirically validate the sharpening mechanism
via inference-time and amortization experiments. We view these findings
as a starting point toward a foundational understanding that can guide
the design and evaluation of self-improvement algorithms.

### 1 Introduction

Contemporary language models are remarkably proficient on a wide range
of natural language tasks ([Brown et al., 2020](#bib.bib19); [Ouyang
et al., 2022](#bib.bib71); [Touvron et al., 2023](#bib.bib93); [OpenAI,
2023](#bib.bib70); [Google, 2023](#bib.bib42)), but inherit shortcomings
of the data on which they were trained. A fundamental challenge is to
achieve better performance than what is directly induced by the
distribution of available, human-generated training data. To this end,
recent work ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al., 2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113)) has raised the
possibility of “self-improvement,” where a model—typically through forms
of self-play or self-training in which the model critiques its own
generations—learns to improve on its own, without external feedback.
This phenomenon is somewhat counterintuitive; at first glance it would
seem to disagree with the well-known data-processing inequality ([Cover,
1999](#bib.bib25)), which implies that no form of self-training should
be able to create information not already in the model. This motivates
the question of why we should expect such supervision-free interventions
will lead to stronger reasoning and planning capabilities.

A dominant hypothesis for why improvement without external feedback
might be possible is that models contain “hidden knowledge” ([Hinton
et al., 2015](#bib.bib48)) that is difficult to access.
Self-improvement, rather than creating knowledge from nothing, is a
means of extracting and distilling this knowledge into a more accessible
form, and thus is a computational phenomenon rather than a statistical
one. While there is a growing body of empirical evidence for this
hidden-knowledge hypothesis ([Furlanello et al., 2018](#bib.bib38);
[Gotmare et al., 2019](#bib.bib43); [Dong et al., 2019](#bib.bib29);
[Abnar et al., 2020](#bib.bib2); [Allen-Zhu and Li, 2020](#bib.bib6)),
particularly in the context of self-distillation, a fundamental
understanding of self-improvement remains missing. Concretely, where in
the model is this hidden knowledge, and when and how can it be
extracted?

#### 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism

Figure 1: Validation of maximum-likelihood sharpening, via Best-of-$`N`$
(BoN) sampling, at inference time. (a) Percent accuracy improvement over
greedy decoding for BoN sharpening with $`N=50`$ on 6 tasks and 7
models, colored by performance. (b) Perecent accuracy improvement over
greedy for BoN sharpening as a function of $`N`$ for 7 different models
on the MATH dataset. (c) Distribution over sequence-level log
probabilities for sampled completions ($`N=1`$) from Phi3.5-Mini on the
MATH dataset, conditioned on whether or not the completion is correct.
Correct completions are noticeably in higher likelihood than incorrect
completions, demonstrating the utility of inference-time sharpening.

In this paper, we posit a potential source of hidden knowledge, and
offer a formal perspective on how to extract it. Our starting point is
the widely observed phenomenon that language models are often better at
verifying whether responses are correct than they are at generating
correct responses ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al., 2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113)). This gap may be
explained by the theory of computational complexity, which suggests that
generating high-quality responses can be less computationally tractable
than verification ([Cook, 1971](#bib.bib24); [Levin, 1973](#bib.bib60);
[Karp, 1972](#bib.bib56)). In autoregressive language modeling,
computing the most likely response for a given prompt is
$`\mathsf{NP}`$-hard in the worst case
([Appendix E](#A5 "Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
whereas the model’s likelihood for a given response can be easily
evaluated.

We view self-improvement as any attempt to narrow this gap, i.e., use
the model as its own verifier to improve generation and _sharpen_ the
model toward high-quality responses. Formally, consider a learner with
access to a base model
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}:\mathcal{X}\to\Delta(\mathcal{Y})`$ representing a
conditional distribution that maps a prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ to a
distribution over responses (i.e., $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$ is
the probability that the model generates the response $`y`$ given the
prompt $`x`$). We posit that $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has already been
trained in some manner (e.g., through next-token prediction or
additional post-training steps such as SFT or RLHF), with the key
feature being that $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ is a good verifier, as
measured by some _self-reward_ function
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid x;\pi_{\texttt{base}})`$ measuring model
certainty. The self-reward function is derived purely from the base
model $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$, without external supervision or feedback.
Examples include normalized and/or regularized sequence likelihood
([Meister et al., 2020](#bib.bib66)), models-as-judges ([Zheng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib118); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113); [Wu et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib103); [Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib98)), and model
confidence ([Wang and Zhou, 2024](#bib.bib99)).

An important special case for sharpening is in language/autoregressive
modeling. Here, we have $`\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{V}^{H}`$ for a vocabulary
space $`\mathcal{V}`$ and sequence length $`H`$, and
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has the autoregressive structure
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)=\prod_{h=1}^{H}\pi_{\texttt{base},h}(y_{h}\mid{}y_{1:h-1},x)`$
for $`y=y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{Y}`$. Sharpening in this setting pertains to
entire responses, i.e., the optimization over responses in
[Equation 2](#S1.E2 "In 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is at the _sequence level_. In contrast, popular decoding strategies
such as greedy, low-temperature sampling, and beam search operate at the
token-level; nevertheless, they can be viewed as heuristics for
_inference-time sharpening_.¹¹ 1 More sophisticated decoding strategies
like normalized/regularized sequence likelihood ([Meister et al.,
2020](#bib.bib66)) or chain-of-thought decoding ([Wang and Zhou,
2024](#bib.bib99)) use various metrics of model “confidence” to guide
sampling in the absence of external verifiers; these too admit an
informal interpretation as “sharpening”. See
[Appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Detailed Discussion of Related Work ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
The combinatorial response space can make sharpening computationally
demanding and so, an appealing alternative to inference-time sharpening
is _amortization via self-training_
([Section 2](#S2 "2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
The latter captures many existing self-training schemes ([Huang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al.,
2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al., 2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al.,
2024](#bib.bib113)), and is the main focus of this paper; we use the
term _sharpening_ without further qualification to refer to the latter.

We refer to the sharpening mechanism as the phenomenon where responses
from a model with the highest certainty (in the sense of large
self-reward $`r_{\texttt{self}}`$) exhibit the greatest performance on a
task of interest. Though it is unclear a-priori whether there are
self-rewards related to task performance, the successes of
self-improvement in prior works ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang
et al., 2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al., 2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang
et al., 2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113)) give strong
positive evidence. These works suggest that, in many settings, models do
have hidden knowledge: the model’s own self-reward correlates with
response quality, but it is computationally challenging to generate high
self-rewarding—and thus high quality—responses. It is the role of
(algorithmic) sharpening to leverage these verifications to improve the
quality of generations, despite computational difficulty.

#### 1.2 Contributions

We initiate the theoretical study of self-improvement via the sharpening
mechanism. We disentangle the choice of self-reward from the algorithms
used to optimize it, and aim to understand: (i) When and how does
self-training achieve sharpening? (ii) What are the fundamental limits
for self-training algorithms?

###### Algorithms for sharpening ([Section 2](#S2 "2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))

The starting point for our work is to consider two natural families of
self-improvement algorithms based on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and
reinforcement learning (RL/RLHF), respectively, SFT-Sharpening and
RLHF-Sharpening. Both algorithms amortize the sharpening objective
[(2)](#S1.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
into a dedicated post-training/fine-tuning phase:

- •
  SFT-Sharpening filters responses where the self-reward
  $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x;\pi_{\texttt{base}})`$ is large and
  fine-tunes on the resulting dataset, invoking common SFT pipelines
  ([Amini et al., 2024](#bib.bib7); [Sessa et al., 2024](#bib.bib85);
  [Gui et al., 2024](#bib.bib45); [Pace et al., 2024](#bib.bib72)).
- •
  RLHF-Sharpening directly applies reinforcement learning techniques
  (e.g., PPO ([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib84)) or DPO ([Rafailov
  et al., 2023](#bib.bib78))) to optimize the self-reward function
  $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x;\pi_{\texttt{base}})`$.

In the remainder of the paper, we introduce a theoretical framework to
analyze the performance of these algorithms, and validate our findings
empirically. Our main contributions are as follows.

###### Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective ([Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))

As a concrete proposal for one source of hidden knowledge, we focus on
self-rewards defined by the model’s sequence-level log-probabilities:

|     |                                                                                                    |     |     |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid x;\pi_{\texttt{base}}):=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)`$ |     | (3) |

This is a stylized self-reward function, which offers perhaps the
simplest objective for self-improvement in the absence of external
feedback (i.e., purely supervision-free), yet also connects
self-improvement to a rich body of theoretical computer science
literature on computational trade-offs for optimization (inference)
versus sampling
([Appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Detailed Discussion of Related Work ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
We view
[Equation 3](#S1.E3 "In Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
as a _clean and minimal objective_ that reveals the interplay between
hidden knowledge, computational bottlenecks, and self-improvement in
generative model. In spite of its simplicity, we show empirically that
maximum-likelihood sharpening is already sufficient to achieve
non-trivial performance gains over greedy decoding on a range of
reasoning tasks with several language models; cf.
[Fig. 1](#S1.F1 "In 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
We believe it can serve as a starting point toward understanding forms
of self-improvement that use more sophisticated self-rewarding or
judging but are less amenable to theoretical analysis ([Huang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al.,
2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al., 2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al.,
2024](#bib.bib113)).

###### A statistical framework for sharpening ([Sections 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Sample Complexity Framework ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism") and [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))

Though the goal of sharpening is computational in nature, we recast
self-training according to the maximum-likelihood sharpening objective
[Eq. 3](#S1.E3 "In Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
as a statistical problem where we aim to produce a model approximating
[(2)](#S1.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
using a polynomial number of (i) sample prompts $`x\sim\mu`$, (ii)
sampling queries of the form $`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)`$, and (iii)
likelihood evaluations of the form $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)`$.
Evaluating the efficiency of the algorithm through the number of such
queries, this abstraction offers a natural way to evaluate the
performance of self-improvement/sharpening algorithms and establish
fundamental limits and minimax optimality, similar to the role of
information-based complexity in optimization ([Nemirovski et al.,
1983](#bib.bib69); [Traub et al., 1988](#bib.bib94); [Raginsky and
Rakhlin, 2011](#bib.bib79); [Agarwal et al., 2012](#bib.bib3)),
statistical query complexity in computational learning theory ([Blum
et al., 1994](#bib.bib16); [Kearns, 1998](#bib.bib57); [Feldman,
2012](#bib.bib33); [Feldman, 2017](#bib.bib34)), and query complexity
more broadly. We use our framework to prove new lower bounds and
fundamental limits which highlight the importance of the base model’s
_coverage_ (that is, probability mass placed on high-quality responses).

###### Analysis of sharpening algorithms ([Section 4](#S4 "4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))

Within our statistical framework for maximum-likelihood sharpening, we
show that SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening provably converge to
sharpened models, establishing several results:

- •
  Optimality of SFT-Sharpening. We show that SFT-Sharpening succeeds at
  learning a sharpened model whenever $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has
  sufficient coverage, and is minimax optimal in a worst-case sense.
  Perhaps surprisingly, we show that a novel variant based on adaptive
  sampling can bypass this lower bound.
- •
  Benefits of RLHF-Sharpening. We show that RLHF-Sharpening also
  succeeds at learning a sharpened model and achieves similar
  performance to SFT-Sharpening when $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has
  sufficient coverage. However, we show that this algorithm can bypass
  the need for coverage—improving over SFT-Sharpening—by leveraging
  deliberate _exploration_ of the response space.

###### Empirical investigation ([Section 5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))

We empirically explore the extent to which our theoretical framework can
aid language models in a variety of tasks. We first consider three
choices of self-reward, including maximum-likelihood sharpening, and
sharpen via a practical approximation, inference-time best-of-N
sampling: given a prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, we draw $`N`$ responses
$`y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$ and return
the response
$`\widehat{y}=\argmax_{y_{i}}r_{\texttt{self}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)`$; this is
equivalent to [Stiennon et al. (2020)](#bib.bib90); [Gao et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib39); [Yang et al. (2024)](#bib.bib110) and is a popular
approach in modern deployments.²² 2 We mention in passing that
inference-time best-of-$`N`$ sampling enjoys provable guarantees for
maximizing the maximum-likelihood sharpening objective when $`N`$ is
sufficiently large. See
[Appendix C](#A3 "Appendix C Guarantees for Inference-Time Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for details. We consider an extensive list of model-dataset pairs and
find that sharpening, even with the stylized maximum-likelihood
self-reward, often improves performance over greedy decoding. We then
implement one of our algorithms, SFT-Sharpening, on a subset of these
model-dataset pairs and observe a significant positive effect on
performance, indicating that sharpening can indeed be amortized. An
overview of our inference-time experiments can be found in
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

#### 1.3 Related Work

Our work is most directly related to a growing body of empirical
research that studies self-training for language models in a
supervision-free setting with no external feedback ([Huang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al.,
2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al., 2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al.,
2024](#bib.bib113)). The specific algorithms for
self-improvement/sharpening we study can be viewed as applications of
standard alignment algorithms ([Amini et al., 2024](#bib.bib7); [Sessa
et al., 2024](#bib.bib85); [Gui et al., 2024](#bib.bib45); [Pace et al.,
2024](#bib.bib72); [Christiano et al., 2017](#bib.bib22); [Bai et al.,
2022a](#bib.bib9); [Ouyang et al., 2022](#bib.bib71); [Rafailov et al.,
2023](#bib.bib78)) with a specific choice of reward function. However,
the maximum likelihood sharpening objective
[(3)](#S1.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
used for our theoretical results has been relatively unexplored within
the alignment and self-improvement literature.

On the theoretical side, current understanding of self-training is
limited. One line of work, focusing on the _self-distillation_ objective
([Hinton et al., 2015](#bib.bib48)) for classification and regression,
aims to provide convergence guarantees for self-training in stylized
setups such as linear models ([Mobahi et al., 2020](#bib.bib67); [Frei
et al., 2022](#bib.bib37); [Das and Sanghavi, 2023](#bib.bib26); [Das
et al., 2024](#bib.bib27); [Pareek et al., 2024](#bib.bib74)), with
[Allen-Zhu and Li (2020)](#bib.bib6) giving guarantees for feedforward
neural networks and [Boix-Adsera (2024)](#bib.bib17) proposing a general
PAC-style framework. To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first
to study self-training in a general framework that subsumes language
modeling.

See
[Appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Detailed Discussion of Related Work ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for a more extensive discussion of related work.

###### Notation

For an integer $`n\in\mathbb{N}`$, we let $`[n]`$ denote the set
$`\{1,\dots,n\}`$. For a set $`\mathcal{X}`$, we let
$`\Delta(\mathcal{X})`$ denote the set of all probability distributions
over $`\mathcal{X}`$. We adopt standard big-oh notation, and write
$`f=\widetilde{O}(g)`$ to denote that
$`f=O(g\cdot{}\max\left\{1,\mathrm{polylog}(g)\right\})`$,
$`a\lesssim{}b`$ as shorthand for $`a=O(b)`$, and $`a\asymp{}b`$ as
shorthand for $`a=\Theta(b)`$.

### 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement

This section introduces the two families of self-improvement algorithms
for sharpening that we study. Going forward, we omit the dependence of
$`r_{\texttt{self}}`$ on $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ when it is clear from
context. We use the notation $`\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}`$ or
$`\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}`$ to denote exact optimization over a
user-specified model class $`\Pi`$ for theoretical results ([Agarwal
et al., 2019](#bib.bib5); [Foster and Rakhlin, 2023](#bib.bib35));
empirically, these operations can be implemented by training a neural
network to low loss.

#### 2.1 Self-Improvement through SFT: SFT-Sharpening

SFT-Sharpening filters responses for which the self-reward
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)`$ is large, and applies standard
supervised fine-tuning on the resulting dataset ([Amini et al.,
2024](#bib.bib7); [Sessa et al., 2024](#bib.bib85); [Gui et al.,
2024](#bib.bib45); [Pace et al., 2024](#bib.bib72)). This can be viewed
as amortizing inference-time sharpening via the effective-but-costly
best-of-$`N`$ sampling approach ([Brown et al., 2024](#bib.bib18);
[Snell et al., 2024](#bib.bib88); [Wu et al., 2024b](#bib.bib104)).
Concretely, suppose we have a collection of prompts
$`x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}`$. For each prompt, we sample $`N`$ responses
$`y_{i,1},\ldots,y_{i,N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x_{i})`$,
then compute the best-of-$`N`$ response
$`y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{i}=\argmax_{j\in[N]}\left\{r_{\texttt{self}}(y_{i,j}\mid{}x_{i})\right\}`$,
scoring via the model’s self-reward function. We compute the sharpened
model via supervised fine-tuning on the best-of-$`N`$ responses:

|     |                                                                                                                          |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}^{\texttt{BoN}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\pi(y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{i}\mid{}x_{i}).`$ |     | (4) |

SFT-Sharpening is a simple, flexible self-training scheme, and converges
to a sharpened model as $`n,N\to\infty`$. In
[Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we consider a variant of SFT-Sharpening based on _adaptive sampling_,
which adjusts the number of sampled responses adaptively for better
performance.

#### 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening

A drawback of the SFT-Sharpening algorithm is that it may ignore useful
information contained in the self-reward function
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)`$. Fixing a regularization parameter
$`\beta>0`$ throughout, our second class of algorithms solve a
KL-regularized reinforcement learning problem in the spirit of RLHF and
other alignment methods ([Christiano et al., 2017](#bib.bib22); [Bai
et al., 2022a](#bib.bib9); [Ouyang et al., 2022](#bib.bib71); [Rafailov
et al., 2023](#bib.bib78)). Defining
$`\En_{\pi}[\cdot]=\En_{x\sim\mu,y\sim\pi(\cdot\mid{}x)}[\cdot]`$ and
$`D_{\mathsf{KL}}\left(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\texttt{base}}\right)=\En_{\pi}\big[\log\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\big]`$,
we choose

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                          |     |     |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\approx\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\left\{\En_{\pi}\left[r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)\right]-\beta D_{\mathsf{KL}}\left(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\texttt{base}}\right)\right\}.`$ |     | (5) |

The exact optimizer
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\left\{\En_{\pi}\left[r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)\right]-\beta D_{\mathsf{KL}}\left(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\texttt{base}}\right)\right\}`$
for this objective has the form

|     |                                                                                                                                                |     |     |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{\beta}(y\mid{}x)\propto\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\cdot\exp\left(\beta^{-1}r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)\right),`$ |     | (6) |

which converges to the solution to the sharpening objective in
[Eq. 2](#S1.E2 "In 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
as $`\beta\to{}0`$. Thus,
[Eq. 5](#S2.E5 "In 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
can be seen to encourage sharpening.

There are many choices for what RLHF/alignment algorithm one might use
to solve
[(5)](#S2.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
For our theoretical results, we implement
[Eq. 5](#S2.E5 "In 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
using an approach inspired by DPO and its reward-based variants
([Rafailov et al., 2023](#bib.bib78); [Gao et al., 2024](#bib.bib40)).
Given a dataset $`\mathcal{D}=\left\{(x,y,y^{\prime})\right\}`$ of $`n`$
examples sampled via $`x\sim\mu`$ and
$`y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$, we consider the
algorithm that solves

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{(x,y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}}\left(\beta\log\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}-\beta\log\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}-\left(r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)-r_{\texttt{self}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)\right)^{2}.`$ |     | (7) |

In the sequel
([Section 4](#S4 "4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
we show that this approach leads to comparable guarantees to
SFT-Sharpening, but that a more sophisticated DPO variant that
incorporates _online exploration_ ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib108)) can
offer provable benefits.

### 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening

This section introduces the theoretical framework within which we will
analyze the SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening algorithms. We first
introduce the maximum-likelihood sharpening objective as a stylized
self-reward function, then introduce our statistical framework for
sharpening.

#### 3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening

Our theoretical results focus on the maximum-likelihood sharpening
objective given by

|     |                                                                                 |     |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid x):=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x),`$ |     | (8) |

which we aim to maximize using conditional samples
$`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$ from the base model. This is
a simple and stylized self-reward function, but we will show that it
enjoys a rich theory. In particular, we can restate the problem of
sharpening with this self-reward through the lens of _amortization_.
