The tacit assumption in this framing is that the maximum-likelihood
response constitutes a useful form of hidden knowledge.
Maximum-likelihood sharpening connects the study of self-improvement to
a large body of research in theoretical computer science demonstrating
computational reductions between optimization (inference) and sampling
(generation) ([Kirkpatrick et al., 1983](#bib.bib58); [Lovász and
Vempala, 2006](#bib.bib63); [Singh and Vishnoi, 2014](#bib.bib87); [Ma
et al., 2019](#bib.bib64); [Talwar, 2019](#bib.bib92)). Our sharpening
framework offers a new learning-theoretic perspective by focusing on the
problem of amortizing this type of reduction.

We evaluate the quality of an approximately sharpened model as follows.
Let

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\vcentcolon={}\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x);
``` |  | (9) |

we interpret $`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\subset\mathcal{Y}`$ as a set to
accommodate non-unique maximizers, and will write $`y^{\star}(x)`$ to
indicate a unique maximizer when it exists (i.e., when
$`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)=\{y^{\star}(x)\}`$).

{definition}

\[Sharpened model\] We say that a model $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is
$`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened relative to $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ if

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}\left(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x\right)\geq{}1-\delta\right]\geq{}1-\epsilon.`$ |  | (10) |

That is, an $`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened model places at least
$`1-\delta`$ mass on arg-max responses on all but an
$`\epsilon`$-fraction of prompts under $`\mu`$. For small $`\delta`$ and
$`\epsilon`$, we are guaranteed that $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is a high-quality
generator: sampling from the model will produce an arg-max response with
high probability for most prompts.

###### Maximum-likelihood sharpening for autoregressive models

Though our most general results are agnostic to the structure of
$`\mathcal{X}`$, $`\mathcal{Y}`$, and $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$, our
primary motivation is the autoregressive setting in which
$`\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{V}^{H}`$ for a *vocabulary space* $`\mathcal{V}`$
and sequence length $`H`$, and where $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has the
autoregressive structure
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)=\prod_{h=1}^{H}\pi_{\texttt{base},h}(y_{h}\mid{}y_{1:h-1},x)`$
for $`y=y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{Y}`$. We observe that when the response
$`y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{H})\in\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{V}^{H}`$ is a sequence
of tokens, the maximum-likelihood sharpening objective
[(3)](#S1.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
sharpens toward the *sequence-level* arg-max response:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\argmax_{y_{1:H}}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x).`$ |  | (11) |

Although somewhat stylized,
[Eq. 11](#S3.E11 "In Maximum-likelihood sharpening for autoregressive models ‣ 3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is a non-trivial (in general, computationally intractable; see
[Appendix E](#A5 "Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
solution concept. We view the sequence-level arg-max as a form of hidden
knowledge that cannot necessarily be uncovered through naive sampling or
greedy decoding.

###### Role of $`\delta`$ for autoregressive models

As can be verified through simple examples, beam-search and greedy
tokenwise decoding do not return an exact (or even approximate) solution
to
[(11)](#S3.E11 "Equation 11 ‣ Maximum-likelihood sharpening for autoregressive models ‣ 3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
in general. There is one notable exception: If the model has already
been sharpened to $`\delta<1/2`$ and the arg-max sequence is unique,
then greedy decoding will succeed. {proposition}\[Greedy decoding
succeeds for sharpened policies\] Let $`\pi=\pi_{1:H}`$ be an
autoregressive model defined over response space
$`\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{V}^{H}`$. For a given prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$,
if $`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)=\left\{y^{\star}(x)\right\}`$ is a singleton and
$`\pi(y^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)>1/2`$, then the greedy decoding strategy that
selects

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{h}=\argmax_{y_{h}\in\mathcal{V}}\pi_{h}(y_{h}\mid{}\widehat{y}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{y}_{h-1},x)`$ |  | (12) |

guarantees that $`\widehat{y}=y^{\star}(x)`$. This result is tight, in
the sense that there exist $`\pi`$ with
$`\pi(y^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1/2`$ for which greedy decoding fails to
recover $`y^{\star}(x)`$. This means that if we start from an
un-sharpened model, it can suffice to focus on sharpening to
$`\delta<1/2`$.

#### 3.2 Sample Complexity Framework

As described, sharpening in the sense of
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is a purely computational problem, which makes it difficult to evaluate
the quality and optimality of self-improvement algorithms. To address
this, we introduce a novel statistical framework for sharpening,
inspired by the success of oracle complexity in optimization
([Nemirovski et al., 1983](#bib.bib69); [Traub et al.,
1988](#bib.bib94); [Raginsky and Rakhlin, 2011](#bib.bib79); [Agarwal
et al., 2012](#bib.bib3)) and statistical query complexity in
computational learning theory ([Blum et al., 1994](#bib.bib16); [Kearns,
1998](#bib.bib57); [Feldman, 2012](#bib.bib33); [Feldman,
2017](#bib.bib34)).

{definition}

\[Sample-and-evaluate framework\] In the sample-and-evaluate framework,
the algorithm designer does not have explicit access to the base model
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$. Instead, they access $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
only through *sample-and-evaluate queries*: The learner is allowed to
sample $`n`$ prompts $`x\sim\mu`$. For each prompt $`x`$, they can
sample $`N`$ responses
$`y_{1},y_{2},\dots y_{N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid x)`$ and
observe the likelihood $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)`$ for each
such response. The efficiency, or *sample complexity*, of the algorithm
is measured through the total number of sample-and-evaluate queries
$`m\vcentcolon={}n\cdot{}N`$. This framework can be seen to capture
algorithms like SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening (implemented with
DPO), which only access the base model $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ through
i) sampling responses via $`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$
(generation), and ii) evaluating the likelihood
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$ (verification) for these responses. We
view the sample complexity $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ as a natural statistical
abstraction for the computational complexity of self-improvement (a
clear parallel to oracle complexity for optimization algorithms), one
which is amenable to information-theoretic lower bounds.³³ 3 Concretely,
the sample complexity $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ is a lower bound on the running
time of any algorithm that operates in the sample-and-evaluate
framework. We will aim to show that, under appropriate assumptions,
SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening can learn an
$`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened model with sample complexity

|     |                                                              |     |      |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       m=\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1},C_{\texttt{prob}})
       ```                                                           |     | (13) |

where $`C_{\texttt{prob}}`$ is a potentially problem-dependent constant.

#### 3.3 Fundamental Limits

Before diving into our analysis of SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening in
the sample-and-evaluate framework, let us take a brief detour to give a
sense for how sample complexity guarantees for sharpening should scale.
To this end, we will prove a lower bound or fundamental limit on the
sample complexity of any algorithm in the sample-and-evaluate framework.

Intuitively, the performance of any sharpening algorithm based on
sampling should depend on how well the base model
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ covers the arg-max response $`y^{\star}(x)`$. To
capture this, we define the following *coverage coefficient*:⁴⁴ 4 This
quantity can be interpreted as a special case of the
$`L_{1}`$-concentrability coefficient ([Farahmand et al.,
2010](#bib.bib32); [Xie and Jiang, 2020](#bib.bib106); [Zanette et al.,
2021](#bib.bib114); [Amortila et al., 2024](#bib.bib8)) studied in the
theory of offline reinforcement learning.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\texttt{cov}}=\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)}\right].`$ |  | (14) |

More generally, for a model $`\pi`$, we define
$`\bm{y}^{\pi}(x)=\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi(y\mid{}x)`$ and
$`C_{\texttt{cov}}(\pi)=\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\pi(\bm{y}^{\pi}(x)\mid{}x)}\right]`$.

Our main lower bound shows that for worst-case choice of $`\Pi`$, the
coverage coefficient acts as a lower bound on the sample complexity of
any sharpening algorithm. {theorem}\[Lower bound for sharpening\] Fix an
integer $`d\geq 1`$ and parameters $`\epsilon\in(0,1)`$ and $`C\geq 1`$.
There exists a class of models $`\Pi`$ such that (i)
$`\log|\Pi|\asymp d(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1}))`$, (ii)
$`\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}C_{\texttt{cov}}(\pi)\lesssim C`$, and (iii)
$`\bm{y}^{\pi}(x)`$ is a singleton for all $`\pi\in\Pi`$,
$`x\in\mathcal{X}`$. Any sharpening algorithm $`\widehat{\pi}`$ that
achieves
$`\En\left[\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}(x)\mid{}x)>1/2]\right]\geq 1-\epsilon`$
for all $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}\in\Pi`$ must collect a total number of
samples $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ at least

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle m\gtrsim\frac{C\log|\Pi|}{\epsilon^{2}\cdot{}(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1}))}.`$ |  | (15) |

This result shows that the complexity of any
$`(\epsilon,1/2-\delta)`$-sharpening algorithm (for $`\delta>0`$) in the
sample-and-evaluate framework must depend polynomially on the coverage
coefficient $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$, as well as the accuracy parameter
$`\epsilon`$. The lower bound also depends on the expressivity of
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$, as captured by the model class complexity term
$`\log\lvert\Pi\rvert`$. We will show in the sequel that it is possible
to match this lower bound. Note that this result also implies a lower
bound for the general sharpening problem (i.e., general
$`r_{\texttt{self}}`$), since maximum-likelihood sharpening is a special
case.

{remark}

\[Relaxed notions of sharpening and coverage\] The notion of coverage in
[Eq. 14](#S3.E14 "In 3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is somewhat stringent, since it requires that $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
place large mass on $`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)`$ on average. In
[Appendix F](#A6 "Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we introduce a more general and permissive notion of approximate
sharpening
([Section F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Guarantees for Approximate Maximizers ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
which allows the model to sharpen toward approximate arg-max responses
(in the sense that
$`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\geq{}(1-\gamma)\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$
for an approximation parameter $`\gamma>0`$). This notion of sharpening
leads to significantly weaker coverage requirements, and we state
generalized versions of all our main results which accommodate this in
the appendix.

We close this section by noting that numerous recent works—focusing on
inference-time computation—show that standard language models exhibit
favorable coverage with respect to desirable responses ([Brown et al.,
2024](#bib.bib18); [Snell et al., 2024](#bib.bib88); [Wu et al.,
2024b](#bib.bib104)). We replicate these findings in our experimental
setup
in [Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
These works suggest that, despite the exponentially large response
space, the coverage coefficient $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ may be small in
standard language modeling tasks.

### 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms

Equipped with the sample complexity framework from
[Section 3](#S3 "3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we now prove that the SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening families of
algorithms provably learn a sharpened model for the maximum likelihood
sharpening objective under natural statistical assumptions.

Throughout this section, we treat the model class $`\Pi`$ as a fixed,
user-specified parameter. Our results—in the tradition of statistical
learning theory—allow for general classes $`\Pi`$, and are agnostic to
the structure beyond standard generalization arguments.

#### 4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening

Recall that when we specialize to the maximum-likelihood sharpening
self-reward, the SFT-Sharpening algorithm takes the form

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}^{\texttt{BoN}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{i}\mid{}x_{i}),
``` |  | (16) |

where
$`y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{i}=\argmax_{j\in[N]}\left\{\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i,j}\mid{}x_{i})\right\}`$
for $`y_{i,1},\ldots,y_{i,N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x_{i})`$.

To analyze SFT-Sharpening, we first make a realizability assumption. Let
$`\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(x)`$ be the distribution of the random variable
$`y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(x)\sim\argmax\left\{\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)\mid{}y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)\right\}`$.
{assumption} The model class $`\Pi`$ satisfies
$`\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}\in\Pi`$. Our main guarantee for SFT-Sharpening
is as follows. {theorem}\[Sample complexity of SFT-Sharpening \] Let
$`\rho,\delta\in(0,1)`$ be given, and suppose we set
$`N=N^{\star}\log(2\delta^{-1})`$ for a parameter
$`N^{\star}\in\mathbb{N}`$. If
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
holds, then for any $`n\in\mathbb{N}`$, SFT-Sharpening produces a model
$`\widehat{\pi}`$ such that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\lesssim{}\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot{}\frac{\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})}{n}+\frac{C_{\texttt{cov}}}{N^{\star}}.`$ |  | (17) |

In particular, given $`(\epsilon,\delta)`$, by setting
$`n=c\cdot\frac{\log\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta\epsilon}`$ and
$`N^{\star}=c\cdot\frac{C_{\texttt{cov}}}{\epsilon}`$ for an appropriate
constant $`c>0`$, we are guaranteed that
$`\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\leq\epsilon`$,
and have total sample complexity

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle m=O\left(\frac{C_{\texttt{cov}}\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta\epsilon^{2}}\right).`$ |  | (18) |

This result shows that SFT-Sharpening, via
[Eq. 18](#S4.E18 "In 4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
is minimax optimal in the sample-and-evaluate framework when $`\delta`$
is constant. In particular, the sample complexity bound in
[Eq. 18](#S4.E18 "In 4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
matches the lower bound in
[Section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
up to polynomial dependence on $`\delta`$ and logarithmic factors.
Whether the $`1/\delta`$ factor in
[Eq. 18](#S4.E18 "In 4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
can be removed is an interesting technical question, but may not be
practically consequential because—as discussed in
[Section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Sample Complexity Framework ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")—the
regime $`\delta<1/2`$ is most meaningful for autoregressive language
modeling.

{remark}

\[On realizability and coverage\] Realizability assumptions such as
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
(which asserts that the class $`\Pi`$ is powerful enough to model the
distribution of the best-of-$`N`$ responses) are standard in learning
theory ([Agarwal et al., 2019](#bib.bib5); [Lattimore and Szepesvári,
2020](#bib.bib59); [Foster and Rakhlin, 2023](#bib.bib35)), though
certainly non-trivial (see
[Appendix E](#A5 "Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for a natural example where they may not hold). The coverage assumption,
while also standard, when combined with the hypothesis that
high-likelihood responses are desirable, suggests that
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ generates high-quality responses with reasonable
probability. In general, doing so may require leveraging non-trivial
*serial* computation at inference time via procedures such as
Chain-of-Thought ([Wei et al., 2022](#bib.bib101)). Although recent work
shows that such serial computation *cannot* be amortized ([Li et al.,
2024](#bib.bib61); [Malach, 2023](#bib.bib65)), SFT-Sharpening instead
amortizes the *parallel* computation of best-of-$`N`$ sampling, and thus
has different representational considerations.

###### Benefits of adaptive sampling

SFT-Sharpening is optimal in the sample-and-evaluate framework, but we
show in
[Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
that a variant which selects the number of responses adaptively based on
the prompt $`x`$ can bypass this lower bound, improving the
$`\epsilon`$-dependence in
[Eq. 18](#S4.E18 "In 4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
from $`\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}`$ to $`\frac{1}{\epsilon}`$.

#### 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening

We now turn our attention to theoretical guarantees for the
RLHF-Sharpening algorithm family, which uses tools from reinforcement
learning to optimize the self-reward function. When specialized to
maximum-likelihood sharpening, the RL objective used by RLHF-Sharpening
takes the form

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\approx\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\left\{\En_{\pi}\left[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\right]-\beta D_{\mathsf{KL}}\left(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\texttt{base}}\right)\right\}`$ |  | (19) |

for $`\beta>0`$. The exact optimizer
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\left\{\En_{\pi}\left[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\right]-\beta D_{\mathsf{KL}}\left(\pi\,\|\,\pi_{\texttt{base}}\right)\right\}`$
for this objective has the form
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}(y\mid{}x)\propto\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y\mid{}x)`$,
which converges to a sharpened model (per
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
as $`\beta\to{}0`$.

The key challenge we encounter in this section is the mismatch between
the RL reward $`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)`$ and the sharpening
desideratum $`\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid x)`$. For example,
suppose a unique argmax—say, $`y^{\star}(x)`$—and second-to-argmax—say,
$`y^{\prime}(x)`$—are nearly as likely under $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$.
Then the RL reward
$`\E_{\widehat{\pi}}[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)]`$ must be
optimized to extremely high precision before $`\widehat{\pi}`$ can be
guaranteed to distinguish the two. To quantify this effect, we introduce
a *margin condition*. {assumption}\[Margin\] For a margin parameter
$`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}>0`$, the base model $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\geq{}(1+\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}})\cdot\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\quad\forall{}y^{\prime}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}(x),\quad\forall{}x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu).
``` |  | (20) |

SFT-Sharpening does not suffer from the pathology in the example above,
because once $`y^{\star}(x)`$ and $`y^{\prime}(x)`$ are drawn in a batch
of $`N`$ responses, we have $`y^{\texttt{BoN}}_{i}=y^{\star}(x_{i})`$
regardless of margin. However, as we shall show in
[Section 4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2 "4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
the RLHF-Sharpening algorithm is amenable to online exploration, which
may improve dependence on other problem parameters.

##### 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization

The first of our theoretical results for RLHF-Sharpening takes an
offline reinforcement learning approach, whereby we implement
[Eq. 5](#S2.E5 "In 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
using a reward-based variant of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO)
([Rafailov et al., 2023](#bib.bib78); [Gao et al., 2024](#bib.bib40)).
Let $`\mathcal{D}_{\texttt{pref}}=\left\{(x,y,y^{\prime})\right\}`$ be a
dataset of $`n`$ examples sampled via $`x\sim\mu`$,
$`y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$. For a parameter
$`\beta>0`$, we consider the algorithm that solves

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\in\argmin_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{(x,y,y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{\texttt{pref}}}\left(\beta\log\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}-\beta\log\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}-\left(\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)-\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)\right)^{2}.`$ |  | (21) |

###### Assumptions

Per [Rafailov et al. (2023)](#bib.bib78), the solution to
[Eq. 21](#S4.E21 "In 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
coincides with that of
[Eq. 3](#S1.E3 "In Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
asymptotically. To provide finite-sample guarantees, we make a number of
statistical assumptions. First, we make a natural realizability
assumption (e.g., [Zhu et al. (2023)](#bib.bib119); [Xie et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib108)). {assumption}\[Realizability\] The model class
$`\Pi`$ satisfies $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\in\Pi`$.⁵⁵ 5 See
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for a discussion of this assumption. Next, we define two concentrability
coefficients for a model $`\pi`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\pi}=\En_{\pi}\left[\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right],\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{\pi^{\prime}}};\beta}\vcentcolon={}\En_{\pi}\left[\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi^{\prime}(y\mid{}x)}\right)^{\beta}\right].`$ |  | (22) |

The following result shows that both coefficients are bounded for the
KL-regularized model $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$. {lemma} The model
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ satisfies
$`\mathcal{C}_{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}\leq C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ and
$`\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}};\beta}\leq{}\left\lvert\mathcal{Y}\right\rvert`$.
Motivated by this result, we assume the coefficients in
[Eq. 22](#S4.E22 "In Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
are bounded for all $`\pi\in\Pi`$. {assumption}\[Concentrability\] All
$`\pi\in\Pi`$ satisfy $`\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\leq C_{\texttt{conc}}`$ for a
parameter $`C_{\texttt{conc}}\geq C_{\texttt{cov}}`$, and
$`\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta}\leq C_{\texttt{loss}}`$
for a parameter $`C_{\texttt{loss}}\geq\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert`$. By
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
this assumption is consistent with
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for reasonable bounds on $`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$ and
$`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$; note that our sample complexity bounds will only
incur logarithmic dependence on $`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$.

###### Main result

Our sample complexity guarantee for RLHF-Sharpening (via
[Eq. 21](#S4.E21 "In 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
is as follows.{theorem} Let $`\epsilon,\delta,\rho\in(0,1)`$ be given.
Set $`\beta\lesssim\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}\delta\epsilon`$, and suppose
that
[Sections 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
[4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
hold with parameters $`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$, $`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$, and
$`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}>0`$. For an appropriate choice for $`n`$, the
DPO algorithm
([Eq. 21](#S4.E21 "In 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
ensures that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,
$`\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\leq\epsilon`$,
and has sample complexity

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle m=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{C_{\texttt{conc}}\log^{3}(C_{\texttt{loss}}\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})}{\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}^{2}\delta^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right).`$ |  | (23) |

Compared to the guarantee for SFT-Sharpening, RLHF-Sharpening learns a
sharpened model with the same dependence on the accuracy $`\epsilon`$,
but a worse dependence on $`\delta`$; as we primarily consider
$`\delta`$ constant (cf.
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Role of 𝛿 for autoregressive models ‣ 3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
we view this as relatively unimportant. We further remark that
RLHF-Sharpening uses $`N=2`$ responses per prompt, while SFT-Sharpening
uses many ($`N\approx C_{\texttt{cov}}/\epsilon`$) responses but fewer
prompts. Other notable differences include:

- •
  RLHF-Sharpening requires the margin condition in
  [Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
  and has sample complexity scaling with
  $`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}^{-1}`$. We believe this dependence is
  natural for algorithms based on reinforcement learning, as it relates
  suboptimality with respect to the reward function
  $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$
  (i.e.,
  $`\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)-\En_{y\sim\widehat{\pi}(x)}\left[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\right]\right]\leq\epsilon`$,
  the objective minimized by reinforcement learning) to approximate
  sharpening error
  $`\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]`$.
  However, it is not clear if the precise dependence we pay is
  necessary.
- •
  RLHF-Sharpening requires a bound on the uniform coverage parameter
  $`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$, which is larger than the parameter
  $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ required by SFT-Sharpening in general. We expect
  that this assumption can be removed by incorporating pessimism in the
  vein of ([Liu et al., 2024](#bib.bib62); [Huang et al.,
  2024](#bib.bib51)). Also, RLHF-Sharpening requires a bound on the
  parameter $`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$. This grants control over the range of
  the reward function $`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$, which can
  otherwise be unbounded. Since the dependence on $`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$
  is only logarithmic, we view this as fairly mild. Overall, the
  guarantee in
  [Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px2 "Main result ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
  may be somewhat pessimistic; it would be interesting if the result can
  be improved to match the sample complexity of SFT-Sharpening.

##### 4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration

The sample complexity guarantees in
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px2 "Main result ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
scale with the coverage parameter
$`C_{\texttt{cov}}=\En[1/\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)|x)]`$,
which in general is unavoidable in the sample-and-evaluate framework via
our lower
bound, [Section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Although $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ is a problem-dependent parameter, in the
worst case it can be as large as $`\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert`$ (which is
exponential in sequence length for autoregressive models). Fortunately,
unlike SFT-Sharpening, the RLHF-Sharpening objective
[(5)](#S2.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is amenable to RL algorithms employing active exploration, leading to
improved sample complexity when the class $`\Pi`$ has additional
structure.

Our below guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening replace the assumption of
bounded coverage with boundedness of a structural parameter for the
model class $`\Pi`$ known as the “sequential extrapolation coefficient”
(SEC) ([Xie et al., 2023](#bib.bib107); [Xie et al.,
2024](#bib.bib108)), which we denote by $`\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)`$. The
formal definition is deferred to
[Section J.2](#A10.SS2 "J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Conceptually, $`\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)`$ may thought of as a generalization
of the eluder dimension ([Russo and Van Roy, 2013](#bib.bib81); [Jin
et al., 2021](#bib.bib55)). It can always be bounded by the coverability
coefficient of the model class ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib108)) and can
be as large as $`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$ in the worst case, so that bounds
based on the SEC reflect improvements that are possible in favorable
instances.

Beyond boundedness of the SEC, we require a bound on the range of the
log-probabilities of $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$.{assumption}\[Bounded
log-probabilities\] For all $`\pi\in\Pi`$,
$`(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}`$,
$`\big\lvert\log\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y|x)}\big\rvert\leq R_{\mathsf{max}}`$.
We expect that the dependence on $`R_{\mathsf{max}}`$ in our result can
be replaced with $`\log(C_{\texttt{loss}})`$
([Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
but we omit this extension to simplify presentation.

We appeal to (a slight modification of) XPO, an iterative language model
alignment algorithm due to [Xie et al. (2024)](#bib.bib108). XPO is
based on the objective in
[Eq. 21](#S4.E21 "In 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
but unlike DPO, incorporates a bonus term to encourage exploration to
leverage online interaction. See
[Section J.2](#A10.SS2 "J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for a detailed overview.

###### Main result

The main guarantee for RLHF-Sharpening with XPO is as follows.

{theorem}

\[Informal version of
[Section J.2.3](#A10.SS2.SSS3 "J.2.3 Applying XPO to maximum-likelihood sharpening ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")\]
Suppose that
[Sections 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2 "4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
hold with parameters $`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}},R_{\mathsf{max}}>0`$,
and that
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
holds with
$`\beta=\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}/(2\log(2|\mathcal{Y}|/\delta))`$. For
any $`m\in\mathbb{N}`$ and $`\rho\in(0,1)`$, XPO
([Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In J.2.2 Guarantees for KL-regularized policy optimization with XPO ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
when configured appropriately, produces an
$`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened model $`\widehat{\pi}\in\Pi`$ with
probability at least $`1-\rho`$, and uses sample complexity⁶⁶ 6
Technically,
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In J.2.2 Guarantees for KL-regularized policy optimization with XPO ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
operates in a slight generalization of the sample-and-evaluate framework
([Section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Sample Complexity Framework ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
where the algorithm is allowed to query
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$ for arbitrary $`x,y`$. We expect that
our lower bound
([Section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
can be extended to to show that dependence on $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ is
necessary in the worst case even in this more general framework.
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In J.2.2 Guarantees for KL-regularized policy optimization with XPO ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is fundamentally using additional instance-dependent structure (via the
SEC) to avoid dependence on the coverage parameter,
$`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
m=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)\cdot{}\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})}{\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}^{2}\delta^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right).
``` |  | (24) |

The takeaway from
[Section 4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2.Px1 "Main result ‣ 4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is that there is no dependence on the coverage coefficient for
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$. Instead, the rate depends on the complexity of
exploration, as governed by the sequential extrapolation coefficient
$`\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)`$. We emphasize that while we present guarantees for
XPO under the sequential extrapolation coefficient for concreteness, we
expect similar guarantees can derived for other active exploration
algorithms and complexity measures ([Jiang et al., 2017](#bib.bib54);
[Foster et al., 2021](#bib.bib36); [Jin et al., 2021](#bib.bib55); [Xie
et al., 2023](#bib.bib107)).

###### Example: Linearly parameterized models

As a stylized example of a model class $`\Pi`$ where active exploration
dramatically improves the sample complexity of sharpening, we consider
the class $`\Pi_{\phi,B}`$ of linear softmax models. This class consists
of models of the form

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(y\mid{}x)\propto\exp(\langle\phi(x,y),\theta\rangle),`$ |  | (25) |

where $`\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is a parameter vector with
$`\lVert\theta\rVert_{2}\leq B`$, and $`\phi(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is
a known feature map with $`\left\|\phi(x,y)\right\|\leq{}1`$. The
sequential extrapolation coefficient for this class can be bounded as
$`\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)=\widetilde{O}(d)`$, and the optimal KL-regularized
model $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ is a linear softmax model (i.e.,
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\in\Pi`$) whenever the base model
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ is itself a linear softmax model. This leads to
the following result.

{theorem}

Fix $`\epsilon,\delta,\rho\in(0,1)`$ and $`B>0`$. Suppose that (i)
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}=\pi_{\theta^{\star}}`$ is a linear softmax model
with
$`\lVert\theta^{\star}\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}B}{3\log(2|\mathcal{Y}|/\delta)}`$;
(ii) $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ satisfies
[Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
with parameter $`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}`$.
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In J.2.2 Guarantees for KL-regularized policy optimization with XPO ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
with base model $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$, reward function
$`r(x,y):=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x,y)`$, and model class
$`\Pi_{\phi,B}`$, returns an $`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened model with
probability at least $`1-\rho`$, and with sample complexity
$`m=\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1},\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}^{-1},d,B,\log(|\mathcal{Y}|/\rho))`$.
Importantly,
[Section 4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2.Px2 "Example: Linearly parameterized models ‣ 4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
has no dependence on the coverage parameter $`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$,
scaling only with the dimension $`d`$ of the softmax model class.

For a quantitative comparison, we note that even for the simple special
case of the linear softmax model class, it is straightforward to
construct examples of models $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ where
$`C_{\texttt{cov}}=\mathbb{E}[1/\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\star}(x)|x)]\asymp|\mathcal{Y}|\asymp\exp(\Omega(d))`$,
yet
[Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is satisfied with $`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}=\Omega(1)`$. For such
models, SFT-Sharpening will incur $`\exp(\Omega(d))`$ sample complexity;
see
[Section J.2.4](#A10.SS2.SSS4 "J.2.4 Application: linear softmax models ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for details. Hence,
[Section 4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2.Px2 "Example: Linearly parameterized models ‣ 4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
represents an *exponential* improvement, obtained by exploiting the
structure of the self-reward function in a way that goes beyond
SFT-Sharpening.

{remark}

\[Non-triviality\]
[Section 4.2.2](#S4.SS2.SSS2.Px2 "Example: Linearly parameterized models ‣ 4.2.2 Benefits of Exploration ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is quite stylized in the sense that if the parameter vector
$`\theta^{\star}`$ of $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ is known, then it is
trivial to directly compute the parameter vector for the sharpened model
$`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ (which corresponds to rescaling
$`\theta^{\star}`$).
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In J.2.2 Guarantees for KL-regularized policy optimization with XPO ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is interesting and non-trivial nonetheless because it *does not have
explicit knowledge of $`\theta^{\star}`$*, as it operates in the
sample-and-evaluate oracle model
([Section 3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Sample Complexity Framework ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
Moreover, the guarantee generalizes to any model class $`\Pi`$ for which
$`\mathsf{SEC}(\Pi)`$ can be bounded; see
[Section J.2.3](#A10.SS2.SSS3 "J.2.3 Applying XPO to maximum-likelihood sharpening ‣ J.2 Proof of and ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for the formal statement.

### 5 Experiments

In this section we explore the sharpening mechanism empirically. We
consider inference-time experiments that demonstrate that
self-improvement through sharpening is possible, as well as
training-time experiments that successfully amortize the cost of
self-improvement, thereby avoiding computational overhead at inference
time. We first describe the general experimental setup, then turn to the
results of our experiments.

#### 5.1 Experimental Setup

We experiment with sharpening using the following models, all of which
(except for gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct) are available on
[https://huggingface.co](https://huggingface.co); we provide HuggingFace
model identifiers below.

1.  1.
    Phi models: We use several models from the Phi family ([Abdin
    et al., 2024](#bib.bib1)), specifically Phi3-Mini
    (“microsoft/Phi-3-mini-4k-instruct”), Phi3-Small
    (“microsoft/Phi-3-small-8k-instruct”), Phi3-Medium
    (“microsoft/Phi-3-medium-4k-instruct”), and Phi3.5-Mini
    (“microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct”).
2.  2.
    Llama3.2-3B-Instruct (“meta-llama/Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct”) ([Dubey
    et al., 2024](#bib.bib30)).
3.  3.
    Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3
    (“mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3”) ([Jiang et al.,
    2023](#bib.bib53)).
4.  4.
    gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct ([Brown et al., 2020](#bib.bib19)): We access
    this model via the OpenAI API.
5.  5.
    llama2-7b-game24-policy-hf
    (“OhCherryFire/llama2-7b-game24-policy-hf”): We use the model of
    [Wan et al. (2024)](#bib.bib96), which is a Llama-2 model finetuned
    on the GameOf24 task ([Yao et al., 2024](#bib.bib111)). We use this
    model only for experiments with GameOf24.

We consider the following tasks:

1.  1.
    GSM8k: We use the above models to generate responses to prompts from
    the GSM-8k dataset ([Cobbe et al., 2021](#bib.bib23)) where the goal
    is to generate a correct answer to an elementary school math
    question. For inference-time experiments, we take the first 256
    examples from the test set in the ‘‘main’’ subset.⁷⁷ 7
    [https://huggingface.co/datasets/openai/gsm8k](https://huggingface.co/datasets/openai/gsm8k).
2.  2.
    MATH: We use the above models to generate responses to prompts from
    the MATH dataset ([Hendrycks et al., 2021](#bib.bib47)), which
    consists of more difficult math questions. For inference-time
    experiments, we consider “all” subsets and take the first 256
    examples of the test set where the solution matches the regular
    expression `(\d*)`.⁸⁸ 8
    [https://huggingface.co/datasets/lighteval/MATH](https://huggingface.co/datasets/lighteval/MATH).
3.  3.
    ProntoQA: We use the above models to generate responses to prompts
    from the ProntoQA dataset ([Saparov and He, 2023](#bib.bib82)),
    which consists of chain-of-thought-style reasoning questions with
    boolean answers. For inference-time experiments, we take the first
    256 examples from the training set.⁹⁹ 9
    [https://huggingface.co/datasets/longface/prontoqa-train](https://huggingface.co/datasets/longface/prontoqa-train).
4.  4.
    MMLU: We use the above models to generate responses to prompts from
    three subsets of the MMLU dataset ([Hendrycks et al.,
    2020](#bib.bib46)), specifically college_biology (Bio),
    college_physics (Phys), and college_chemistry (Chem), all of which
    consist of multiple choice questions.¹⁰¹⁰ 10
    [https://huggingface.co/datasets/cais/mmlu](https://huggingface.co/datasets/cais/mmlu).
    For inference-time experiments, we take the first 256 examples of
    the test set for each subset.
5.  5.
    GameOf24: We use only the model of [Wan et al. (2024)](#bib.bib96)
    (i.e., llama2-7b-game24-policy-hf), on the GameOf24 task ([Yao
    et al., 2024](#bib.bib111)). The prompts are four numbers and the
    goal is to combine the numbers with standard arithmetic operations
    to reach the number ‘24.’ For inference-time experiments, we use
    both the train and test splits of the dataset.¹¹¹¹ 11
    [https://github.com/princeton-nlp/tree-of-thought-llm/tree/master/src/tot/data/24](https://github.com/princeton-nlp/tree-of-thought-llm/tree/master/src/tot/data/24)

All of our experiments were run on 40G NVIDIA A100 GPUs, 192G AMD MI300X
GPUs, or through the OpenAI API.

#### 5.2 Validation of Inference-Time Sharpening

We first validate the sharpening mechanism (i.e., the phenomenon that
responses from a model with high self-reward $`r_{\texttt{self}}`$ enjoy
high performance on downstream tasks) through inference-time
experiments, focusing on the maximum likelihood self-reward. For each
(model, task) pair, we sample $`N`$ generations per prompt with
temperature 1 and return the best of the $`N`$ generations according to
the maximum-likelihood sharpening self-reward function
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$; we
compare against greedy decoding as a baseline, whose accuracy is
displayed in
[Figure 3](#S5.F3 "In 5.3 Inference-Time Sharpening with other Self-Reward Functions ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

###### Implementation details

For all models and datasets except for GameOf24, we used 1-shot
prompting to ensure that models conform to the desired output format and
to elicit chain of thought reasoning (for GameOf24 we do not provide a
demonstration in the prompt). We set the maximum length of decoding to
be $`512`$ tokens. We used 10 seeds for all (model, task) pairs with a
maximum value of $`N=50`$ in Best-of-$`N`$ sampling. We simulated $`N`$
responses for $`N<50`$ by subsamplng the 50 generated samples. For
Best-of-$`N`$ sampling, we always use temperature $`1.0`$. Since greedy
decoding is a deterministic strategy, there is no need to average over
multiple seeds for each (model, task) pair. In all experiments, we
collect both the responses and their log-likelihoods under the
*reference model* (i.e., the original model from which samples were
generated).

###### Results

We display our findings in
[Figure 1](#S1.F1 "In 1.1 Our Perspective: The Sharpening Mechanism ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")(a)
and in
[Figure 2](#S5.F2 "In Results ‣ 5.2 Validation of Inference-Time Sharpening ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism");
because we only consider a single model for GameOf24, we separate the
results for this task into
[Figure 4](#A1.F4 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
We visualize performance—measured through normalized accuracy
improvement over greedy decoding. We also visualize log-likelihoods
(under $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$) of the selected responses in
[Fig. 5](#A1.F5 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
We find that:

1.  1.
    Across all (model, task) pairs, inference-time Best-of-$`N`$
    sharpening (using
    $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$)
    improves over naïve sampling with temperature 1.0.
2.  2.
    For all datasets, Best-of-$`N`$ sharpening improves upon standard
    *greedy decoding*, for at least one model.
3.  3.
    Analogously, for every model, there is at least one dataset for
    which Best-of-$`N`$ sharpening improves over greedy decoding.

We further explore the relationship between sequence-level
log-probabilities and generation quality in
[Figure 6](#A1.F6 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
where we plot the empirical distributions of responses sampled with
temperature 1 from the base model for a variety of model-dataset pairs,
conditioned on whether or not the response is correct. We find that *the
distribution of log probabilities conditioned on correctness
stochastically dominates the distribution conditioned on incorrectness*
in each (model, task) pair evaluated, which provides more evidence that
maximum likelihood sharpening represent a reasonable self-improvement
target.

We mention several other observations from the experiments. First, in
most cases, performance and log-likelihood saturate at relatively small
values of $`N`$, typically around 10 or 20. This suggests that
significant improvements can be obtained with relatively low
computational overhead. Second, in some cases, performance can degrade
as $`N`$ increases. We found that this happens for two reasons: (1) the
performance of the reference model is poor and so $`r_{\texttt{self}}`$
does not provide a good signal (e.g., with Llama3.2-3B-Instruct) and (2)
the Best-of-$`N`$ criteria selects for short responses, which have
higher log-likelihood but cannot leverage the computational and
representational benefits of chain-of-thought, thereby yielding worse
performance (e.g., with gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct on GSM8k).

Figure 2: Percent lift in accuracy of inference-time BoN-sharpening over
greedy decoding in each task as $`N`$ is varied. For many task-model
pairs, the accuracy improves as $`N`$ increases, demonstrating the
efficacy of maximum likelihood sharpening.

#### 5.3 Inference-Time Sharpening with other Self-Reward Functions

Although we focus on
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$
throughout the paper, the sharpening framework is significantly more
general.¹²¹² 12 Although we do not experiment with model-as-judge
approaches ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52), e.g.,), which obtain
self-reward by re-prompting, we note that they can be cast in the
sharpening framework by simply defining the self reward
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)`$ to be the output of the model when
prompted with a verification/scoring prompt, the original prompt $`x`$
and the candidate response $`y`$. As such, we experiment with
inference-time sharpening for other choices for $`r_{\texttt{self}}`$:

1.  1.
    Length-normalized log-likelihood:
    $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\frac{1}{|y|}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$
    where $`|y|`$ is the length, in tokens, of the response.
2.  2.
    Majority (self-consistency): All datasets except GameOf24 have
    multiple-choice, boolean, or numerical answers. Although we allow
    responses to contain chain-of-thought tokens, we can extract the
    answer from each response and use the most-frequently-occuring
    answer. This can be seen as a sample-based approximation to the
    following self-reward function:
    $`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\sum_{y^{\prime}:y^{\prime}_{\texttt{ans}}=y_{\texttt{ans}}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)`$,
    where $`y_{\texttt{ans}}`$ are the “answer” tokens in the full
    response $`y`$.

Finally, as a skyline we consider the *coverage* criterion ([Brown
et al., 2024](#bib.bib18)), where we simply check if any of the sampled
responses corresponds to the correct answer. This criterion is a skyline
and does not fit into sharpening framework, as it uses knowledge of the
ground truth (external) task reward function.

Results are displayed in
[Figure 3](#S5.F3 "In 5.3 Inference-Time Sharpening with other Self-Reward Functions ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
For length-normalized log-likelihood (a) and majority (b), we see
qualitatively similar behavior to (unnormalized) log-likelihood:
inference-time sharpening via these self-reward functions offers
improvement over both vanilla (temperature 1.0) sampling and greedy
decoding. In both cases, the improvements are generally larger than
those obtained with log-likelihood. Finally, examining the coverage
criteria, we see that with $`N=50`$ samples, all of the models almost
always produce a correct answer on all tasks, raising the possibility of
other self-reward functions that further improve performance.

Figure 3: Performance of alternative self-reward functions for
inference-time BoN sharpening: Percent accuracy improvement over greedy
decoding for (a) BoN sharpening with length-normalized log probability
and (b) majority voting, with both demonstrating efficacy on a range of
model-task pairs. (c) Coverage of correct answer, a skyline
demonstrating that most model-task pairs produce the correct answer in
at least one completion out of 50 for most prompts. (d) Accuracy of
greedy decoding baseline on each model-task pair.

#### 5.4 Training-Time Sharpening with SFT-Sharpening

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
| Model | Dataset | % Lift over Greedy (Accuracy) | Lift over Greedy (Likelihood) |
| Phi3.5-Mini | MATH | $`19.24\pm 2.41`$ | $`48.33\pm 0.17`$ |
| Phi3.5-Mini | GSM8k | $`1.82\pm 0.64`$ | $`1.49\pm 0.55`$ |
| Phi3.5-Mini | ProntoQA | $`12.46\pm 1.08`$ | $`5.64\pm 0.01`$ |
| Mistral-7B | MATH | $`8.88\pm 5.55`$ | $`5.71\pm 3.00`$ |

Table 1: Experimental results for SFT-Sharpening

In addition to inference-time experiments, we also evaluate
training-time sharpening, and demonstrate empirically that
SFT-Sharpening effectively amortizes inference-time BoN. Due to limited
computational resources, we restrict our attention to a subset of the
model-task pairs considered in
[Section 5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Validation of Inference-Time Sharpening ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
that have particularly promising inference-time BoN performance. For
each pair, we evaluate the performance of SFT-Sharpening as a means to
amortize the inference-time cost of multiple generations.

For each of the chosen model-dataset pairs (cf.
[Table 1](#S5.T1 "In 5.4 Training-Time Sharpening with SFT-Sharpening ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
we sample $`N=50`$ responses with temperature 1 for each prompt in the
dataset and select the most likely (according to the relevant reference
model). We then combine these likely responses with the prompts in order
to form a training corpus and train with the SFT-Sharpening objective.
We apply Low Rank Adaptation ([Hu et al., 2021](#bib.bib49)) to the
model, sweeping over LoRA rank, learning rate scheduler, and weight
decay in order to return the best optimized model.¹³¹³ 13 In all
experiments involving Phi3.5-Mini we use a batch size of 4;
unfortunately, due to a known numerical issue with LoRA on
Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 involving batch size $`>1`$, we use a batch of
1 in this case. Because of this choice, instead of the 30 epochs we use
to train our other models, for Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, we run only 10
epochs. We report the specific hyperparameters chosen in
[Table 2](#A1.T2 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
On all models, we used a learning rate of $`3\times 10^{-4}`$ with
linear decay to zero and gradient clamping at 0.1.

###### Results

[Table 1](#S5.T1 "In 5.4 Training-Time Sharpening with SFT-Sharpening ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
reports our results for SFT-Sharpening. We report best model checkpoint
during training for each model-dataset pair, averaged across 3 random
seeds, with responses are sampled with temperature 1 from the fine-tuned
model. We report (i) the percent lift in accuracy on the dataset with
respect to the greedy generation of the reference model; and (ii) the
increase in average sequence level log-likelihood with respect to the
same. For all model-dataset pairs, we observe improvement on both
metrics, demonstrating that some amortization is possible with
SFT-Sharpening.

Next, in
[Figures 7](#A1.F7 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [8](#A1.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
([Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
we display the evolution of the metrics in
[Table 1](#S5.T1 "In 5.4 Training-Time Sharpening with SFT-Sharpening ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
throughout training for each model-dataset pair. In
[Fig. 7](#A1.F7 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we find that while Phi3.5-Mini is quite well-behaved on MATH and
ProntoQA, the training curve for GSM8k is quite noisy. The
log-probability appears to be a significantly less useful proxy for
accuracy on this dataset than for the others; similar phenomena were
observed in [Block et al. (2023)](#bib.bib15) in a variety of tasks. In
[Fig. 8](#A1.F8 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we find that for Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 on MATH, we achieve
improvement after training for sufficiently long, but the optimization
suffers an substantial initial drop and spends $`\sim 90\%`$ of the
gradient steps recovering before improvement is observed; we speculate
that this is a function of insufficient hyper-parameter tuning for the
optimization itself, rather than a fundamental barrier.

Finally, in
[Figure 9](#A1.F9 "In Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
([Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A Additional Experimental Results ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
we investigate the effect of the parameter $`N`$ on the performance of
SFT-Sharpening for Phi3.5-Mini on MATH. In particular, in forming our
training set, we choose $`N\in\{10,25,50\}`$ and repeat the procedure
described above, averaging our results over three seeds. We find that
increasing $`N`$ leads to a modest increase in the sequence-level
log-likelihood, in accordance with our theory, and a consequent increase
in the accuracy of the fine-tuned model.

### 6 Conclusion

We view our theoretical framework for sharpening as a starting point
toward a foundational understanding of self-improvement that can guide
the design and evaluation of algorithms. To this end, we raise several
directions for future research.

- •
  *Representation learning.* A conceptually appealing feature of our
  framework is that it is agnostic to the structure of the model under
  consideration, but an important direction for future work is to study
  the dynamics of self-improvement for specific models/architectures and
  understand the representations that these models learn under
  self-training.
- •
  *Richer forms of self-reward.* Our theoretical results study the
  dynamics of self-training in a stylized framework where the model uses
  its own log-probabilities as a self-reward. Empirical research on
  self-improvement leverages more sophisticated approaches (e.g.,
  specific prompting techniques) ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52);
  [Wang et al., 2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al., 2022b](#bib.bib10);
  [Pang et al., 2023](#bib.bib73); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113)) and
  it is important to understand when and how these forms of
  self-improvement are beneficial.

## Part I Additional Discussion and Results

### Appendix A Additional Experimental Results

In this section we display omitted figures discussed in
[Section 5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

|                          |          |              |           |     |
|--------------------------|----------|--------------|-----------|-----|
| Model                    | Dataset  | Weight Decay | LoRA Rank |     |
| Phi3.5-Mini              | MATH     | 0.1          | 16        |     |
| Phi3.5-Mini              | GSM8k    | 0.5          | 16        |     |
| Phi3.5-Mini              | ProntoQA | 0.0          | 16        |     |
| Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 | MATH     | 1.0          | 8         |     |

Table 2: Hyperparameters for training-time sharpening experiments with
SFT-Sharpening.

Figure 4: Effect of inference-time BoN-sharpening on GameOf24 with
finetuned llama2-7b-game24-policy-hf model from [Wan et al.
(2024)](#bib.bib96).

Figure 5: Effect of $`N`$ on average sequence level log-probabilities
for inference-time BoN-sharpening on various model-task pairs, compared
to greedy decoding baseline. As predicted by theory, the likelihood of
sequences sampled with BoN-sharpening increases with $`N`$.

Figure 6: Distribution of sequence-level log-probabilities for responses
sampled with temperature 1, conditioned on whether or not the response
is correct. We consider four model-dataset pairs: (a) (Phi3.5-Mini,
MATH); (b) (Phi3.5-Mini, GSM8k); (c) (Phi3.5-Mini, ProntoQA); (d)
(Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, MATH). In all cases except perhaps (c),
conditioning on correctness of the response leads to a noticeable
increase in log-probabilities, further justifying the use of
sequence-level log-probabilities as a self-reward for self-improvement.

Figure 7: Evolution of Phi3.5-Mini under SFT-Sharpening ($`N=50`$) on
different datasets, as measured by (i) % lift over Greedy in accuracy;
and (ii) difference in average sequence-level log-probability of
generated responses under the reference model. The fine-tuned model
learns to produce generations with high probability under the reference
model, and consequently enjoys an increase in accuracy compared to the
base model. However, the model does not fully reach the performance of
inference-time BoN sharpening.

Figure 8: Evolution of Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3 under SFT-Sharpening
($`N=50`$) on MATH, as measured by (i) % lift over Greedy in accuracy;
and (ii) difference in average sequence-level log-probability of
generated responses under the reference model.

Figure 9: Effect of $`N`$ on SFT-Sharpening for Phi3.5-Mini on MATH. We
report (a) % lift in accuracy over greedy; and (b) lift in
sequence-level log-likelihood (averaged over the dataset). In both
cases, we see that increasing $`N`$ leads to greater lift, in accordance
with theory.

### Appendix B Detailed Discussion of Related Work

In this section, we discuss related work in greater detail, including
relevant works not already covered.

###### Self-improvement and self-training

Our work is most directly related to a growing body of empirical
research that studies self-improvement/self-training for language models
in a supervision-free setting in which there is no external
feedback ([Huang et al., 2022](#bib.bib52); [Wang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib100); [Bai et al., 2022b](#bib.bib10); [Pang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib73)), and takes a first step toward providing a
theoretical understanding for these methods. There is also a closely
related body of research on “LLM-as-a-Judge” techniques, which
investigates approaches to designing self-reward functions
$`r_{\texttt{self}}`$, often based on specific prompting techniques
([Zheng et al., 2024](#bib.bib118); [Yuan et al., 2024](#bib.bib113);
[Wu et al., 2024a](#bib.bib103); [Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib98)).

A somewhat complementary line of research develops algorithms based on
self-training and self-play ([Zelikman et al., 2022](#bib.bib115); [Chen
et al., 2024](#bib.bib21); [Wu et al., 2024c](#bib.bib105); [Qu et al.,
2024](#bib.bib77)), but leverages various forms of external feedback
(e.g., positive examples for SFT or explicit reward signal). These
methods typically outperform feedback-free self-improvement
methods ([Zelikman et al., 2022](#bib.bib115)). However, in many
scenarios, obtaining external feedback can be costly or laborious; it
may require collecting high-quality labeled/annotated data, rewriting
examples in a formal language, etc. Thus, these two approaches are not
directly comparable.

We also mention that the self-improvement problem we study is related to
a classical line of research on *self-distillation* ([Buciluǎ et al.,
2006](#bib.bib20); [Hinton et al., 2015](#bib.bib48); [Devlin,
2018](#bib.bib28); [Pham et al., 2021](#bib.bib75); [Rizve et al.,
2021](#bib.bib80)), but this specific form of self-training has received
limited investigation in the context of language modeling.

###### Entropy minimization

Sharpening is also closely related to a line of work on *entropy
minimization* or *minimum entropy regularization*, where we seek models
that have high predictive accuracy and low entropy/uncertainty. This
line of work originated in the semi-supervised learning
literature ([Grandvalet and Bengio, 2004](#bib.bib44)) and was
popularized as a test-time adaptation method in computer vision ([Wang
et al., 2020](#bib.bib97); [Press et al., 2024](#bib.bib76), c.f.,).
Maximum-likelihood sharpening, especially via RL, is closely related in
that
[Equation 5](#S2.E5 "In 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
with $`\beta\to 0`$ and $`r_{\texttt{self}}=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
maximizes $`\En_{\pi}[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)]`$ rather than
$`-H(\pi)=\En_{\pi}[\log\pi(y\mid{}x)]`$. (It is important that the
latter is optimized continuously with $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ as an
initialization, but when this is done it can be seen to sharpen
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$, at least heuristically.) Prior work in this
direction is largely empirical, focused on computer vision domains with
small output spaces $`\mathcal{Y}`$, and hence studies statistical
benefits of entropy minimization. In contrast, we initiate a theoretical
study of sharpening, are primarily motivated by applications to language
modeling with exponentially large output spaces, and view sharpening
primarily as a computational phenomena. However, it would be interesting
to understand whether statistical benefits observed in computer vision
translate to the language modeling setting.

###### Alignment and RLHF

The specific algorithms for self-improvement/sharpening we study can be
viewed as special cases of standard alignment algorithms, including
classical RLHF methods ([Christiano et al., 2017](#bib.bib22); [Bai
et al., 2022a](#bib.bib9); [Ouyang et al., 2022](#bib.bib71)), direct
alignment ([Rafailov et al., 2023](#bib.bib78)), and (inference-time or
training-time) best-of-$`N`$ methods ([Amini et al., 2024](#bib.bib7);
[Sessa et al., 2024](#bib.bib85); [Gui et al., 2024](#bib.bib45); [Pace
et al., 2024](#bib.bib72)). However, the maximum likelihood sharpening
objective
[(3)](#S1.E3 "Equation 3 ‣ Maximum-likelihood sharpening objective () ‣ 1.2 Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
used for our theoretical results has been relatively unexplored within
the alignment literature.

###### Inference-time decoding

Many inference-time decoding strategies such as greedy/low-temperature
decoding, beam-search ([Meister et al., 2020](#bib.bib66)), and
chain-of-thought decoding ([Wang and Zhou, 2024](#bib.bib99)) can be
viewed as instances of inference-time sharpening for specific choices of
the self-reward function $`r_{\texttt{self}}`$. More sophisticated
inference-time search strategies such tree search and MCTS ([Yao et al.,
2024](#bib.bib111); [Wan et al., 2024](#bib.bib96); [Mudgal et al.,
2023](#bib.bib68); [Zhao et al., 2024](#bib.bib117)) are also related,
though this line of work frequently makes use of external reward signals
or verification, which is somewhat complementary to our work.

###### Theoretical guarantees for self-training

On the theoretical side, current understanding of self-training is
limited. One line of work, focusing on the *self-distillation* objective
([Hinton et al., 2015](#bib.bib48)) for binary classification and
regression, aims to provide convergence guarantees for self-training in
stylized setups such as linear models ([Mobahi et al.,
2020](#bib.bib67); [Das and Sanghavi, 2023](#bib.bib26); [Das et al.,
2024](#bib.bib27); [Pareek et al., 2024](#bib.bib74)), with [Allen-Zhu
and Li (2020)](#bib.bib6) giving guarantees for feedforward neural
networks. Perhaps most closely related to our work is [Frei et al.
(2022)](#bib.bib37), who show that self-training on a model’s
pseudo-labels can amplify the margin for linear logistic regression.
However, to the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to study
self-training in a general framework that subsumes language modeling.

Our results for RLHF-Sharpening are related to a body of work that
provides sample complexity guarantees for alignment methods ([Zhu
et al., 2023](#bib.bib119); [Xiong et al., 2023](#bib.bib109); [Ye
et al., 2024](#bib.bib112); [Huang et al., 2024](#bib.bib51); [Liu
et al., 2024](#bib.bib62); [Song et al., 2024](#bib.bib89); [Xie et al.,
2024](#bib.bib108)), but our results leverage the structure of the
maximum-likelihood sharpening self-reward function
$`r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x)=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$, and
provide guarantees for the sharpening objective in
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
instead of the usual notion of reward suboptimality used in
reinforcement learning theory.

Lastly, we mention that our results—particularly our *amortization*
perspective on self-improvement—are related to work that studies
representational advantages afforded by additional inference time
([Malach, 2023](#bib.bib65); [Li et al., 2024](#bib.bib61)). These work
focus on truly sequential tasks, while our work focuses on the
complementary question of amortizing *parallel* computation. Thus the
representational implications are quite different.

###### Optimization versus sampling

The maximum-likelihood sharpening objective we introduce in
[Section 3](#S3 "3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
connects the study of *self-improvement* to a large body of research in
theoretical computer science on computational tradeoffs (e.g.,
separations and equivalences) between optimization and sampling
([Barahona, 1982](#bib.bib11); [Kirkpatrick et al., 1983](#bib.bib58);
[Lovász and Vempala, 2006](#bib.bib63); [Singh and Vishnoi,
2014](#bib.bib87); [Ma et al., 2019](#bib.bib64); [Talwar,
2019](#bib.bib92); [Eldan et al., 2022](#bib.bib31)). On the one hand,
this line of research highlights that there exist natural classes of
distributions for which sampling is tractable, yet maximum likelihood
optimization is intractable, and vice-versa. On the other hand, various
works in this line of research also demonstrate *computational
reductions* between optimization and sampling, whereby optimization can
be reduced to sampling and vice-versa.

Our setting indeed includes natural model classes where one should not
expect there to be a computational reduction from optimization
($`\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$) to sampling
($`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$), and hence inference-time
sharpening is computationally intractable
([Section E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Computational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
Of course, coverage assumptions eliminate this intractability. For
training-time sharpening (where the goal is to *amortize* across prompts
by training a sharpened model, as formulated in
[Section 3](#S3 "3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
the obstacle in natural, concrete model classes is not just
computational but in fact *representational*
([Section E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Representational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
Regarding the latter point, we note that while amortized Bayesian
inference has received extensive investigation empirically ([Beal,
2003](#bib.bib12); [Gershman and Goodman, 2014](#bib.bib41); [Swersky
et al., 2020](#bib.bib91); [Bengio et al., 2021](#bib.bib13); [Hu
et al., 2023](#bib.bib50)), we are unaware of theoretical guarantees
outside of this work.

### Appendix C Guarantees for Inference-Time Sharpening

In this section, we give theoretical guarantees for the inference-time
best-of-$`N`$ sampling algorithm for sharpening described in
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
under the maximum-likelihood sharpening self-reward function

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
r_{\texttt{self}}(y\mid{}x;\pi_{\texttt{base}})=\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x).
``` |  | (26) |

Recall that given a prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, the inference-time
best-of-$`N`$ sampling algorithm draws $`N`$ responses
$`y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$, then return
the response
$`\widehat{y}=\argmax_{y_{i}}\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)`$. We
show that this algorithm returns an approximate maximizer for the
maximum-likelihood sharpening objective whenever the base policy
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ has sufficient coverage. For a parameter
$`\gamma\in[0,1)`$ we define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\vcentcolon={}\left\{y\mid{}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\geq{}(1-\gamma)\cdot\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)\right\}`$ |  | (27) |

as the set of $`(1-\gamma)`$-approximate maximizers for
$`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$ (see
[Section F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Guarantees for Approximate Maximizers ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
for background on $`\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$).

{proposition}

Let a prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$ be given. For any $`\rho\in(0,1)`$ and
$`\gamma\in[0,1)`$, as long as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N\geq\frac{\log(\rho^{-1})}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)},`$ |  | (28) |

inference-time best-of-$`N`$ sampling produces a response
$`\widehat{y}\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$ with probability at least
$`1-\rho`$.

Proof of
[Appendix C](#A3 "Appendix C Guarantees for Inference-Time Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Fix a prompt $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, failure probability $`\rho\in(0,1)`$,
and parameter $`\gamma\in(0,1)`$. By definition of the set
$`\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$,
$`\widehat{y}\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$ if and only if there exists
$`i\in[N]`$ such that $`y_{i}\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$. The
complement of this event, i.e., that
$`y_{i}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$ for all $`i\in[N]`$, has
probability

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(y_{i}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x),{}\forall i\in[N]\right)=\left(1-\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\right)^{N}.`$ |  | (29) |

Rearranging the right-hand side, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left(1-\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)\right)^{N}=`$ | $`\displaystyle~\exp\left(-N\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)}\right)\right)\leq\exp\left(-N\cdot\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)\right),`$ |  | (30) |

since $`\log(x)\geq 1-\frac{1}{x}`$ for $`x>0`$, which implies that
$`\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)}\right)\geq\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)`$.
Thus, as long as
$`N\geq\frac{\log(\rho^{-1})}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)}`$,
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
````
