````math
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(y_{i}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x),{}\forall i\in[N]\right)\leq\exp\left(-N\cdot\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}\mid{}x)\right)\leq\exp(-\log(\rho^{-1}))=\rho.`$ |  | (31) |

We conclude that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$, there exists
$`i\in[N]`$ such that $`y_{i}\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$, and
$`\widehat{y}\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$ as a result.

∎

### Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling

SFT-Sharpening is a simple and natural self-training scheme, and
converges to a sharpened policy as $`n,N\to\infty`$. However, using a
fixed response sample size $`N`$ may be wasteful for prompts where the
model is confident. To this end, in this section we introduce and
analyze, a variant of SFT-Sharpening based on *adaptive sampling*, which
adjusts the number of sampled responses adaptively.

###### Algorithm

We present the adaptive SFT-Sharpening algorithm only for the special
case of the maximum likelihood sharpening self-reward. Let a *stopping
parameter* $`\mu>0`$ be given. For $`x_{i}\in\mathcal{X}`$, and
$`y_{i,1},y_{i,2}\ldots\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x_{i})`$,
define a stopping time (e.g., [Benjamini and Hochberg
(1995)](#bib.bib14)) via:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N_{\mu}(x_{i}):=\inf\left\{k:\frac{1}{\max_{1\leq j\leq k}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i,j}\mid{}x_{i})}\leq\frac{k}{\mu}\right\}.`$ |  | (32) |

The adaptive SFT-Sharpening algorithm computes adaptively sampled
responses $`y^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}_{i}`$ via

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
y^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}_{i}\sim\argmax\left\{\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i,j}\mid{}x_{i})\mid{}y_{i,1},\ldots,y_{i,N_{\mu}(x_{i})}\right\},
``` |  | (33) |

then trains the sharpened model through SFT:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\widehat{\pi}^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\pi(y^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}_{i}\mid{}x_{i}).
``` |  | (34) |

Critically, by using scheme in
[Eq. 32](#A4.E32 "In Algorithm ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
this algorithm can stop sampling responses for the prompt $`x_{i}`$ if
it becomes clear that the confidence is large.

###### Theoretical guarantee

We now show that adaptive SFT-Sharpening enjoys provable benefits over
its non-adaptive counterpart through the dependence on the accuracy
parameter $`\epsilon>0`$.

Given $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, and
$`y_{1},y_{2}\ldots\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)`$, let
$`N_{\mu}(x):=\inf big\{k:\frac{1}{\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)}\leq k/\mu big\}`$,
and define a random variable
$`y^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}(x)\sim\argmax\left\{\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y_{i}\mid{}x)\mid{}y_{1},\ldots,y_{N_{\mu}}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)\right\}`$.
Let $`\pi^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}_{\mu}(x)`$ denote the distribution over
$`y^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}(x)`$. We make the following realizability
assumption. {assumption} The model class $`\Pi`$ satisfies
$`\pi^{\texttt{AdaBoN}}_{\mu}\in\Pi`$. Compared to SFT-Sharpening, we
require a somewhat stronger coverage coefficient given by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{\texttt{cov}}=\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right].`$ |  | (35) |

This definition coincides with
[Eq. 14](#S3.E14 "In 3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
when the arg-max response is unique, but is larger in general.

Our main theoretical guarantee for adaptive SFT-Sharpening is as
follows. {theorem} Let $`\delta,\rho\in(0,1)`$ be given. Set
$`\mu=\ln(2\delta^{-1})`$, and assume
[Appendix D](#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px2 "Theoretical guarantee ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
holds. Then with probability at least $`1-\rho`$, the adaptive
SFT-Sharpening algorithm has

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta]\lesssim\frac{\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\delta n},`$ |  | (36) |

and has sample complexity
$`\En[m]=n\cdot\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{\texttt{cov}}\log(\delta^{-1})`$.
Taking $`n\gtrsim\frac{\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{\delta\epsilon}`$ ensures
that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,
$`\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid x)\leq 1-\delta]\leq\epsilon`$,
and gives total sample complexity

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En\left[m\right]=O\left(\frac{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{\texttt{cov}}\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta\epsilon}\right).`$ |  | (37) |

Compared to the result for SFT-Sharpening in
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
this shows that adaptive SFT-Sharpening achieves sample complexity
scaling with $`\frac{1}{\epsilon}`$ instead of
$`\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}`$. We believe the dependence on
$`\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{\texttt{cov}}`$
for this algorithm is tight, as the adaptive stopping rule used in the
algorithm can be overly conservative when
$`\lvert\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\rvert`$ is large.

###### A matching lower bound

We now prove a complementary lower bound, which shows that the
$`\epsilon`$-dependence in
[Appendix D](#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px2 "Theoretical guarantee ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is tight. To do so, we consider the following adaptive variant of the
sample-and-evaluate framework. {definition}\[Adaptive
sample-and-evaluate framework\] In the Adaptive Sample-and-Evaluate
framework, the learner is allowed to sample $`n`$ prompts $`x\sim\mu`$,
and sample an arbitrary, adaptively chosen number of samples
$`y_{1},y_{2},\dots\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid x)`$ before
sampling a new prompt $`x^{\prime}\sim\mu`$. In this framework we define
sample complexity $`m`$ as the total number of pairs $`(x,y)`$ sampled
by the algorithm, which is a random variable. Our main lower bound is as
follows. {theorem}\[Lower bound for sharpening under adaptive sampling\]
Fix an integer $`d\geq 1`$ and parameters $`\epsilon\in(0,1)`$ and
$`C\geq 1`$. There exists a class of models $`\Pi`$ such that (i)
$`\log|\Pi|\eqsim d(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1}))`$, (ii)
$`\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}C_{\texttt{cov}}(\pi)\lesssim C`$, and (iii)
$`\bm{y}^{\pi}(x)`$ is a singleton for all $`\pi\in\Pi`$, for which any
sharpening algorithm $`\widehat{\pi}`$ in the adaptive
sample-and-evaluate framework that achieves
$`\En\left[\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}(x)\mid{}x)>1/2]\right]\geq 1-\epsilon`$
for all $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}\in\Pi`$ must collect a total number of
samples $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ at least

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En[m]\gtrsim\frac{C\log|\Pi|}{\epsilon\cdot{}(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1}))}.`$ |  | (38) |

[Appendix D](#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "A matching lower bound ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is a special case of a more general theorem,
[Appendix H](#A8 "Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
which is stated and proven in
[Appendix H](#A8 "Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

### Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening

In this section, we make several basic observations about the inherent
computational and representational challenges of maximum-likelihood
sharpening. First, in
[Section E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Computational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we focus on computational challenges, and show that computing a
sharpened response for a given prompt $`x`$ can be computationally
intractable in general, even when sampling
$`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$ can be performed efficiently.
Then, in
[Section E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Representational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we shift our focus to representational challenges, and show that even if
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ is an autoregressive model, the “sharpened”
version of $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ may not be representable as an
autoregressive model with the same architecture. These results motivate
the statistical assumptions (coverage and realizability) made in our
analysis of SFT-Sharpening and RLHF-Sharpening in
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

To make the results in this section precise, we work in perhaps the
simplest special case of autoregressive language modelling, where the
model class consists of *multi-layer linear softmax models*. Formally,
let $`\mathcal{X}`$ be the space of prompts, and let
$`\mathcal{Y}:=\mathcal{V}^{H}`$ be the space of responses, where
$`\mathcal{V}`$ is the vocabulary space and $`H`$ is the horizon. For a
collection of fixed/known $`d`$-dimensional feature mappings
$`\phi_{h}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{V}^{h}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ and a
norm parameter $`B`$, we define the model class $`\Pi_{\phi,B,H}`$ as
the set of models

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)=\prod_{h=1}^{H}\pi_{\theta_{h}}(y_{h}\mid{}x,y_{1:h-1})`$ |  | (39) |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\theta}(y_{h}\mid{}x,y_{1:h-1})\propto\exp(\langle\phi(x,y_{1:h}),\theta_{h}\rangle)
``` |  | (40) |

and $`\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{H})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{H}`$ is
any tuple with $`\lVert\theta_{h}\rVert_{2}\leq B`$ for all $`h\in[H]`$.

#### E.1 Computational Challenges

Given query access to $`\phi`$, for any given parameter vector
$`\theta`$ and prompt $`x`$, *sampling* from a linear softmax model
$`\pi_{\theta}`$
([Eq. 39](#A5.E39 "In Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"))
is computationally tractable, since it only requires time
$`\mathrm{poly}(H,|\mathcal{V}|,d)`$. Similarly, *evaluating*
$`\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)`$ for given prompt $`x`$ and response
$`y_{1:H}`$ is computationally tractable. However, the following
proposition shows that computing the sharpened response
$`\argmax_{y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{V}^{H}}\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)`$ for
a given parameter $`\theta`$ and response $`x`$ is $`\mathsf{NP}`$-hard.
Hence, even inference-time sharpening is computationally intractable in
the worst case.

{proposition}

Set $`\mathcal{X}=\{\perp\}`$ and $`\mathcal{V}=\{-1,1\}`$. Set
$`d=d(H):=H+H^{2}+H^{3}`$. Identifying $`[d]`$ with
$`[H]\sqcup[H]^{2}\sqcup[H]^{3}`$, we define
$`\phi_{h}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{V}^{h}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ by
$`\phi_{h}(\perp,y_{1:h})_{i}=y_{i}`$ and
$`\phi_{h}(\perp,y_{1:h})_{(i,j)}=y_{i}y_{j}`$ and
$`\phi_{h}(\perp,y_{1:h})_{(i,j,k)}=y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}`$. There is a
function $`B(H)\leq\mathrm{poly}(H)`$ such that the following problem is
$`\mathsf{NP}`$-hard: given $`\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{H})`$
with $`\max_{h\in[H]}\lVert\theta_{h}\rVert_{2}\leq B(H)`$, compute any
element of
$`\argmax_{y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{V}^{H}}\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}x)`$.
Note that our results in
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and
[Appendix C](#A3 "Appendix C Guarantees for Inference-Time Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
bypass this hardness through the assumption that the coverage parameter
$`C_{\texttt{cov}}`$ is bounded.

Proof of
[Section E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Computational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Fix $`H`$ and recall that $`d(H)=H+H^{2}+H^{3}`$. We define three
collection of basis vectors: $`\left\{e_{h}\right\}_{h\in[H]}`$ cover
the first $`H`$ coordinates,
$`\left\{e_{(h,h^{\prime})}\right\}_{h,h^{\prime}\in[H]^{2}}`$ cover the
next $`H^{2}`$ coordinates, and
$`\left\{e_{(h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime})}\right\}_{h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\in[H]^{3}}`$
cover the last $`H^{3}`$ coordinates. Suppose we define
$`\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{H-2}=0`$, so that
$`\pi_{\theta}(y_{h}|x,y_{1:h-1})=1/2`$ for all $`1\leq h\leq H-2`$.
Define $`\theta_{H-1}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq H-2}J_{ij}e_{(i,j,H-1)}`$ for
a matrix $`J\in\mathbb{R}^{(H-2)\times(H-2)}`$ to be specified later,
and define $`\theta_{H}=\frac{B}{2}(e_{(H-1,H)}+e_{H})`$. Then
$`2^{H-2}\cdot\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)\leq 1/2`$ for any
$`y_{1:H}`$ with $`y_{H-1}=-1`$ or $`y_{H}=-1`$, since this implies that
$`\pi_{\theta_{H}}(y_{H}\mid{}\perp,y_{1:H-1})\leq{}1/2`$. Meanwhile,
for any $`y_{1:H}`$ with $`y_{H-1}=y_{H}=1`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
2^{H-2}\cdot\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)=\frac{\exp\left(\sum_{i,j\leq{}H-2}J_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}\right)}{\exp\left(\sum_{i,j\leq{}H-2}J_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}\right)+\exp\left(-\sum_{i,j\leq{}H-2}J_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}\right)}\cdot\frac{\exp(B)}{\exp(B)+\exp(-B)}.
``` |  | (41) |

Let $`G`$ be any graph on vertex set $`[H-2]`$ and let $`J=-A(G)`$ where
$`A(G)`$ is the adjacency matrix of $`G`$. Then among $`y_{1:H}`$ with
$`y_{H-1}=y_{H}=1`$, $`2^{H-2}\cdot\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)`$ is
maximized when $`y_{1:H-2}`$ corresponds to a max-cut in $`G`$. If $`G`$
has an odd number of edges, then some max-cut removes strictly more than
half of the edges, and for the corresponding sequence $`y_{1:H}`$ we
have
$`2^{H-2}\cdot\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)\geq(1/2+\Omega(1))\cdot(1-\exp(-\Omega(B)))`$,
which is greater than $`1/2`$ when we take $`B:=H`$ and $`H`$ is
sufficiently large. Thus, computing
$`\argmax_{y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{V}^{H}}\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)`$
yields a max-cut of $`G`$. It is well-known that computing a max-cut in
a graph is $`\mathsf{NP}`$-hard, and the assumption that $`G`$ has an
odd number of edges is without loss of generality. ∎

#### E.2 Representational Challenges

To give provable guarantees for our sharpening algorithms, we required
certain *realizability* assumptions, which in particular posited that
the model class actually contains a “sharpened” version of
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
([Sections 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")).
In the simple example of a *single-layer* linear softmax model classes
(corresponding to $`H=1`$ in the above definition),
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is in fact satisfied, and the sharpened model can be obtained by
increasing the temperature of $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$. However,
multi-layer linear softmax models with $`H\gg 1`$ are more realistic.
The following proposition shows that as soon as $`H\geq 2`$, multi-layer
linear softmax model classes may not be closed under sharpening. This
illustrates a potential drawback of training-time sharpening compared to
inference-time sharpening, which requires no realizability assumptions.
It also provides a simple example where greedy decoding does not yield a
sequence-level arg-max response (since increasing temperature in a
multi-layer softmax model class exactly converges to the greedy
decoding).

{proposition}

Let $`\mathcal{X}=\{\perp\}`$, $`\mathcal{V}=[n]`$, and $`H=d=2`$. For
any $`n`$ sufficiently large, there is a multi-layer linear softmax
policy class $`\Pi_{\phi,B,H}`$ and a policy
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}\in\Pi_{\phi,B,H}`$ such that
$`y^{\star}_{1:H}:=\argmax_{y_{1:H}\in\mathcal{V}^{H}}\pi_{\theta}(y_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)`$
is unique, but for all $`B^{\prime}>B`$ and
$`\pi\in\Pi_{\phi,B^{\prime},H}`$, it holds that
$`\pi(y^{\star}_{1:H}\mid{}\perp)\leq 1/2`$.

Proof of
[Section E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Representational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Throughout, we omit the dependence on the prompt $`\perp`$ for
notational clarity. Since $`H=2`$, the model class consists of models
$`\pi_{\theta}`$ of the form

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\theta}(a)=\pi_{\theta_{1}}(y_{1})\pi_{\theta_{2}}(y_{2}\mid{}y_{1})=\frac{\exp(\langle\phi_{1}(y_{1}),\theta_{1}\rangle)}{Z_{\theta_{1}}}\frac{\exp(\langle\phi_{2}(y_{1:2}),\theta_{2}\rangle)}{Z_{\theta_{2}}(y_{1})}\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:softmax-h2}
``` |  | (42) |

for
$`Z_{\theta_{1}}\vcentcolon={}\sum_{y_{1}\in\mathcal{V}}\exp(\langle\phi_{1}(y_{1}),\theta_{1}\rangle)`$
and
$`Z_{\theta_{2}}(y_{1})\vcentcolon={}\sum_{y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}}\exp(\langle\phi_{2}(y_{1:2}),\theta_{2}\rangle)`$.

Define $`\phi_{1}`$ by:

|     |                                                 |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \phi_{1}(i)=\begin{cases}e_{1}&\text{ if }i=1\\
       e_{1}&\text{ if }i=2\\
       e_{2}&\text{ if }i\geq 3\end{cases}.
       ```                                              |     | (43) |

Define $`\phi_{2}`$ by:

|     |                                                       |     |      |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \phi_{2}(i,j)=\begin{cases}e_{1}&\text{ if }i=2,j=1\\
       e_{2}&\text{ if }i=2,j\neq 1\\
       0&\text{ if }i\neq 2\end{cases}.
       ```                                                    |     | (44) |

Define $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}:=\pi_{\theta^{\star}}`$ where
$`\theta^{\star}_{1}:=\theta^{\star}_{2}:=B\cdot{}e_{1}`$ for a
parameter $`B\geq\log(n)`$. Then
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(1)=\pi_{\texttt{base}}(2)`$ and
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(i)\leq e^{-B}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(2)`$ for all
$`i\in\{3,\dots,n\}`$. Moreover,
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid{}i)=\mathsf{Unif}([n])`$ for all
$`i\neq 2`$, and
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(j\mid{}2)\leq e^{-B}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(1\mid{}2)`$
for all $`j\neq 1`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\texttt{base}}(2,1)=\pi_{\texttt{base}}(2)\pi_{\texttt{base}}(1\mid{}2)\geq\frac{1}{2+(n-2)e^{-B}}\cdot\frac{1}{1+(n-1)e^{-B}}\geq\Omega(1)
``` |  | (45) |

whereas $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(i,j)=O(1/n)`$ for all $`(i,j)\neq(2,1)`$.
Thus, $`(2,1)`$ is the sequence-level argmax for sufficiently large
$`n`$. However, for any $`\pi_{\theta}`$ of the form described in
[Eq. 42](#A5.E42 "In E.2 Representational Challenges ‣ Appendix E Computational and Representational Challenges in Sharpening ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\theta}(2,1)\leq\pi_{\theta}(2)\leq\frac{\pi_{\theta}(2)}{\pi_{\theta}(1)+\pi_{\theta}(2)}=\frac{1}{2}
``` |  | (46) |

since $`\phi(1)=\phi(2)`$. This means that there is no $`B^{\prime}`$
for which $`\Pi_{\phi,B^{\prime},H}`$ contains an
$`(\epsilon,\delta)`$-sharpened policy for $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ for
any $`\delta>1/2`$. ∎

## Part II Proofs

### Appendix F Preliminaries

#### F.1 Guarantees for Approximate Maximizers

Recall that the theoretical guarantees for sharpening algorithms in
[Section 4](#S4 "4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
provide convergence to the set
$`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\vcentcolon={}\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$
of (potentially non-unique) maximizers for the maximum-likelihood
sharpening self-reward function $`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$.
These guarantees require that the base model $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$
places sufficient provability mass on $`\bm{y}^{\star}(x)`$, which may
not always be realistic. To address this, throughout this appendix we
state and prove more general versions of our theoretical results that
allow for approximate maximizers, and consequently enjoy weaker coverage
assumptions

For a parameter $`\gamma\in[0,1)`$ we define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\vcentcolon={}\left\{y\mid{}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\geq{}(1-\gamma)\cdot\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)\right\}`$ |  | (47) |

as the set of $`(1-\gamma)`$-approximate maximizers for
$`\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)`$. We quantify the quality of a
sharpened model as follows. {definition}\[Sharpened model\] We say that
a model $`\widehat{\pi}`$ is $`(\epsilon,\delta,\gamma)`$-sharpened
relative to $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ if

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}\left(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x\right)\geq{}1-\delta\right]\geq{}1-\epsilon.`$ |  | (48) |

That is, an $`(\epsilon,\delta,\gamma)`$-sharpened policy places at
least $`1-\delta`$ mass on $`(1-\gamma)`$-approximate arg-max responses
on all but an $`\epsilon`$-fraction of prompts under $`\mu`$.

Lastly, we will make use of the following generalized coverage
coefficient

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}=\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\right],`$ |  | (49) |

which has $`C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}\leq{}C_{\texttt{cov}}`$.

#### F.2 Technical Tools

For a pair of probability measures $`\mathbb{P}`$ and $`\mathbb{Q}`$
with a common dominating measure $`\omega`$, Hellinger distance is
defined via

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\right)=\int\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\omega}}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\omega.`$ |  | (50) |

{lemma}

\[MLE for conditional density estimation (e.g., [Wong and Shen
(1995)](#bib.bib102); [van de Geer (2000)](#bib.bib95); [Zhang
(2006)](#bib.bib116))\] Consider a conditional density
$`\pi^{\star}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{Y})`$. Let
$`\mathcal{D}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}`$ be a dataset in which
$`(x_{i},y_{i})`$ are drawn i.i.d. as
$`x_{i}\sim\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})`$ and
$`y_{i}\sim\pi^{\star}(\cdot\mid{}x)`$. Suppose we have a finite
function class $`\Pi\subset(\mathcal{X}\to\Delta(\mathcal{Y}))`$ such
that $`\pi^{\star}\in\Pi`$. Define the maximum likelihood estimator

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{\pi}\vcentcolon=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{D}}\log\pi(y\mid{}x).`$ |  | (51) |

Then with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\widehat{\pi}(\cdot\mid{}x),\pi^{\star}(\cdot\mid{}x)\right)\right]\leq\frac{2\log(|\Pi|\rho^{-1})}{n}.`$ |  | (52) |

{lemma}

\[Elliptic potential lemma\] Let $`\lambda,K>0`$, and let
$`A_{1},\dots,A_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}`$ be positive semi-definite
matrices with $`\Tr(A_{t})\leq K`$ for all $`t\in[T]`$. Fix
$`\Gamma_{0}=\lambda I_{d}`$ and
$`\Gamma_{t}=\lambda I_{d}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}A_{i}`$ for $`t\in[T]`$. Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Tr(\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}A_{t})\leq\frac{dK\log\frac{(T+1)K}{\lambda}}{\lambda\log(1+K/\lambda)}.
``` |  | (53) |

Proof of
[Section F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Technical Tools ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Fix $`t\in[T]`$. Since $`\Tr(A_{t})\leq 1`$, there is some
$`p_{t}\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})`$ such that
$`A_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim p_{t}}[aa^{\top}]`$ and
$`\mathbb{P}[\lVert a\rVert_{2}\leq 1]=1`$. Now observe that

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\log\det(\Gamma_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1}+A_{t})`$ |  | (54) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1})+\log\det(I_{d}+\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2}A_{t}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2})`$ |  | (55) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1})+\log\det\left(\mathbb{E}_{a\sim p_{t}}\left[I_{d}+\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2}aa^{\top}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2}\right]\right)`$ |  | (56) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1})+\mathbb{E}_{a\sim p_{t}}\log\det(I_{d}+\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2}aa^{\top}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1/2})`$ |  | (57) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1})+\mathbb{E}_{a\sim p_{t}}\log(1+a^{\top}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}a).`$ |  | (58) |

Now $`a^{\top}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}a\leq 1/\lambda`$ with probability $`1`$,
where $`\lambda=\lambda_{\min}(\Gamma_{0})`$. We know that
$`\lambda x\log(1+1/\lambda)\leq\log(1+x)`$ for all
$`x\in[0,1/\lambda]`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log\det(\Gamma_{t})\geq\log\det(\Gamma_{t-1})+\lambda\log(1+1/\lambda)\mathbb{E}_{a\sim p_{t}}a^{\top}\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}a.
``` |  | (59) |

Summing over $`t\in[T]`$, we get

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\log\det(\Gamma_{T})\geq\log\det(\Gamma_{0})+\lambda\log(1+1/\lambda)\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Tr(\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}A_{t}).
``` |  | (60) |

Finally note that $`\lambda_{\max}(\Gamma_{T})\leq T+1`$ so
$`\log\det(\Gamma_{T})\leq d\log T`$, whereas
$`\log\det(\Gamma_{0})\geq d\log\lambda`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Tr(\Gamma_{t-1}^{-1}A_{t})\leq\frac{d\log\frac{T+1}{\lambda}}{\lambda\log(1+1/\lambda)}
``` |  | (61) |

as claimed. ∎

{lemma}

\[Freedman’s inequality, e.g. [Agarwal et al. (2014)](#bib.bib4)\] Let
$`(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{T}`$ be a martingale difference sequence adapted to
filtration $`(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t=0}^{T-1}`$. Suppose that
$`|Z_{t}|\leq R`$ holds almost surely for all $`t`$. For any
$`\delta\in(0,1)`$ and $`\eta\in(0,1/R)`$, it holds with probability at
least $`1-\delta`$ that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}\leq\eta\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]+\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\eta}.
``` |  | (62) |

{corollary}

Let $`(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{T}`$ be a sequence of random variables adapted to
filtration $`(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t=0}^{T-1}`$. Suppose that
$`Z_{t}\in[0,R]`$ holds almost surely for all $`t`$. For any
$`\delta\in(0,1)`$, it holds with probability at least $`1-\delta`$ that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}+4R\log(1/\delta).
``` |  | (63) |

Proof of
[Section F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Technical Tools ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Observe that for any $`t\in[T]`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(Z_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}])^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]`$ |  | (64) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq R\cdot\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}].`$ |  | (65) |

Applying
[Section F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Technical Tools ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
to the sequence
$`(\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]-Z_{t})_{t=1}^{T}`$, which is a
martingale difference sequence with elements supported almost surely on
$`[-R,R]`$, we get for any $`\eta\in(0,1/R)`$ that with probability at
least $`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]-Z_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\eta\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[(Z_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}])^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]+\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\eta}`$ |  | (66) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq\eta R\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]+\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{\eta}.`$ |  | (67) |

Set $`\eta=1/(2R)`$. Simplifying gives

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}+4R\log(1/\delta).
``` |  | (68) |

as claimed. ∎

### Appendix G Proofs from [Section 3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

Proof of
[Section 3.1](#S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2 "Role of 𝛿 for autoregressive models ‣ 3.1 Maximum-Likelihood Sharpening ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
We prove the result by induction. Fix $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, and let
$`y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{H}\vcentcolon={}y^{\star}(x)`$. Fix
$`h\in[H]`$, and assume by induction that
$`\widehat{y}_{h^{\prime}}=y^{\star}_{h^{\prime}}`$ for all
$`h^{\prime}<h`$. We claim that in this case,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{h}(y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}\widehat{y}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{y}_{h-1},x)=\pi_{h}(y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{h-1},x)>1/2,`$ |  | (69) |

which implies that $`\widehat{y}_{h}=y^{\star}_{h}`$. To see this, we
observe that by Bayes’ rule,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi(y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{H}\mid{}x)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\pi(y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}x)`$ |  | (70) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\prod_{h^{\prime}=1}^{h}\pi_{h^{\prime}}(y^{\star}_{h^{\prime}}\mid{}y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{h^{\prime}-1},x)\leq{}\pi_{h}(y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{h-1},x).`$ |  | (71) |

If we were to have
$`\pi_{h}(y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}\widehat{y}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{y}_{h-1},x)=\pi_{h}(y^{\star}_{h}\mid{}y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{h-1},x)\leq{}1/2`$,
it would contradict the assumption that
$`\pi(y^{\star}_{1},\ldots,y^{\star}_{H}\mid{}x)>1/2`$. This proves the
result. ∎

### Appendix H Proofs from [Section 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

Below, we state and prove a generalization of
[Sections 3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Fundamental Limits ‣ 3 A Statistical Framework for Sharpening ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [D](#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "A matching lower bound ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
which allows for approximate maximizers in the sense of
[Section F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Guarantees for Approximate Maximizers ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
as well as a more general coverage coefficient.

To state the result, for a model $`\pi`$, we define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\bm{y}^{\pi}_{\gamma}(x)=\left\{y\mid{}\pi(y\mid{}x)\geq{}(1-\gamma)\cdot\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi(y\mid x)\right\}.`$ |  | (72) |

Next, for any integer $`p\in\mathbb{N}`$, we define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}(\pi)=\left(\En\left[\frac{1}{(\pi(\bm{y}^{\pi}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x))^{p}}\right]\right)^{1/p},`$ |  | (73) |

with the convention that
$`C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}=C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}(\pi_{\texttt{base}})`$.
Our most general lower bound,
[Appendix H](#A8 "Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
holds in the regime where $`\gamma=1/2`$, and thus the best
response$`y`$ has bounded margin away from suboptimal responses.
{theorem}\[Lower bound for sharpening\] Fix integers $`d\geq 1`$ and
$`p\geq 1`$ and parameters $`\epsilon\in(0,1)`$ and $`C\geq 1`$, and set
$`\gamma=1/2`$. There exists a class of models $`\Pi`$ such that i)
$`\log|\Pi|\asymp d(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1/p}))`$, ii)
$`\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}(\pi)\lesssim C`$, and iii)
$`\bm{y}^{\pi}_{\gamma}(x)`$ is a singleton for all $`\pi\in\Pi`$, for
which any sharpening algorithm $`\widehat{\pi}`$ that attains
$`\En\left[\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}_{\gamma}(x))>1/2]\right]\geq 1-\epsilon`$
for all $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}\in\Pi`$ must collect a total number of
samples $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ at least

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle m\gtrsim\begin{cases}\frac{C\log|\Pi|}{\epsilon^{1+1/p}(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1/p}))}&\text{sample-and-evaluate oracle},\\
\frac{C\log|\Pi|}{\epsilon^{1/p}(1+\log(C\epsilon^{-1/p}))}&\text{adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle}.\end{cases}`$ |  | (74) |

###### Proof of [Appendix H](#A8 "Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

Let parameters $`d,p\in\mathbb{N}`$ and $`\epsilon>0`$ be given, and set
$`\gamma=1/2`$. Let $`M\in\mathbb{N}`$ and $`\Delta>0`$ be parameters to
be chosen later. Let $`\mathcal{X}=\{x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{d}\}`$ and
$`\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{0},y_{1},\dots,y_{M}\}`$ be arbitrary discrete sets
(with $`\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert=d+1`$ and
$`\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert=M+1`$).

###### Construction of prompt distribution and model class

We use the same construction for the non-adaptive and adaptive lower
bounds in the theorem statement. We define the prompt distribution
$`\mu`$ via

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mu:=(1-\Delta)\updelta_{x_{0}}+\frac{\Delta}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\updelta_{x_{i}},`$ |  | (75) |

where $`\updelta_{x}`$ denotes the Dirac delta distribution on element
$`x`$.

As the first step toward constructing the model class $`\Pi`$, we
introduce a family of distributions $`(P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{M})`$ on
$`\mathcal{Y}`$ as follows

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P_{0}=\updelta_{y_{0}},\quad\forall i\geq 1,~P_{i}=\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)M}\updelta_{y_{i}}+\sum_{j\in[M]\setminus\{i\}}\frac{1}{M}\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{(M-1)(1-\gamma)}\right)\updelta_{y_{j}}.`$ |  | (76) |

Next, for or any index
$`\mathcal{I}=(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{d})\in[M]^{d}`$, define a model

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi^{\mathcal{I}}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}P_{0}&i=0\\
P_{j_{i}}&i>0\end{cases}.`$ |  | (77) |

We define the model class as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Pi:=\{\pi^{\mathcal{I}}:\mathcal{I}\in[M]^{d}\},`$ |  | (78) |

which we note has

|     |                            |                            |     |      |
|-----|----------------------------|----------------------------|-----|------|
|     | $`\displaystyle\log|\Pi|`$ | $`\displaystyle=d\log M.`$ |     | (79) |

###### Preliminary technical results

Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x):=\{y:\pi^{\mathcal{I}}(y\mid x)\geq(1-\gamma)\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi^{\mathcal{I}}(y\mid{}x)\}.`$ |  | (80) |

The following property is immediate. {lemma} Let
$`\mathcal{I}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})\in[d]^{M}`$. Then
$`\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})=\{y_{j_{i}}\}`$ if $`i>0`$, and
$`\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x_{0})=\{y_{0}\}`$. In view of this
result, we define
$`y^{\mathcal{I}}(x)=\argmax_{y}\pi^{\mathcal{I}}(y\mid{}x)`$ as the
unique arg-max response for $`x`$.

Going forward, let us fix the algorithm under consideration. Let
$`\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}[\cdot]`$ denote the law over the dataset used
by the algorithm when the true instance is $`\pi^{\mathcal{I}}`$
(including possible randomness and adaptivity from the algorithm
itself), and let $`\En^{\mathcal{I}}[\cdot]`$ denote the corresponding
expectation. The following lemma is a basic technical result.
{lemma}\[Reduction to classification\] Let $`\widehat{\pi}`$ be the
model produced by an algorithm with access to a (adaptive)
sample-and-evaluate oracle for $`\pi^{\mathcal{I}}`$. Suppose that for
some $`\epsilon\geq{}0`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)>1/2]\geq 1-\epsilon.`$ |  | (81) |

Define $`\widehat{\mathcal{I}}=(\widehat{j}_{1},\dots,\widehat{j}_{d})`$
via $`\widehat{j}_{i}=\argmax_{j}\widehat{\pi}(y_{j}\mid x_{i})`$, and
write $`\mathcal{I}=(j_{1}^{\star},\dots,j_{d}^{\star})`$. Then,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\leq\epsilon/\Delta.`$ |  | (82) |

Proof of
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "Preliminary technical results ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
As established in
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "Preliminary technical results ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
under instance $`\mathcal{I}`$,
$`\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x_{i})=\{y_{j_{i}^{\star}}\}`$ for any
$`i\in[d]`$. Thus, whenever
$`\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x_{i}))>1/2`$,
$`j_{i}^{\star}=\argmax_{j}\widehat{\pi}(y_{j}\mid x_{i})=:\widehat{j}_{i}`$.
The result follows by noting that the event
$`\{\exists i\in[d]:x=x_{i}\}`$ occurs with probability at least
$`\Delta`$ under $`x\sim\mu`$. ∎

###### Lower bound under sample-and-evaluate oracle

Recall that in the non-adaptive framework, the sample complexity $`m`$
is fixed. In light of
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "Preliminary technical results ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
it suffices to establishes the following claim. {lemma} There exists a
universal constant $`c>0`$ such that for all $`M\geq 8`$, if
$`m\leq cdM/\Delta`$, then
$`\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\geq 1/8`$
for all $`i`$. With this, the result follows by selecting
$`\Delta=16\epsilon`$, with which
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "Preliminary technical results ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
implies that any algorithm with
$`\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)>1/2]\geq{}1-\epsilon`$
must have $`m\gtrsim dM/\Delta`$. To conclude, we choose
$`M\asymp 1+C\epsilon^{-1/p}`$, which gives
$`m\asymp dM/\Delta\asymp dC\epsilon^{-(1+1/p)}\asymp\epsilon^{-(1+1/p)}\log\Pi/\log(1+C\epsilon^{1/p})`$.
Finally, we check that with this choice, all $`\pi\in\Pi`$ satisfy

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}(\pi)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[x=x_{0}\right]+\left(M(1-\gamma)\right)^{p}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[x\neq x_{0}\right]\right)^{1/p}`$ |  | (83) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\left((1-\Delta)+\left(M(1-\gamma)\right)^{p}\Delta\right)^{1/p}`$ |  | (84) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\lesssim\left((1-\Delta)+\left(8C(1-\gamma)\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim C.`$ |  | (85) |

Proof of
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px4 "Lower bound under sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Let $`i\in[d]`$ be fixed. Of the $`m=n\cdot{}N`$ tuples
$`(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x))`$ that are observed by the
algorithm, let $`m_{i}`$ denote the (random) number of such examples for
which $`x=x_{i}`$. From Markov’s inequality, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[m_{i}\leq 2\Delta m/d]\geq\frac{1}{2}`$ |  | (86) |

Going forward, let
$`\mathcal{D}=\left\{(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x))\right\}`$
denote the dataset collected by the algorithm, which has
$`\lvert\mathcal{D}\rvert=m`$. Let $`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$ denote the event
that, for prompt $`x=x_{i}`$, (i) there are at least two distinct
responses $`y_{j}`$ for which $`(x_{i},y_{j})\notin\mathcal{D}`$; and
(ii) there are no pairs $`(x_{i},y)\in\mathcal{D}`$ for which
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x_{i})>\frac{1}{M}`$. Since
$`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$ is a measurable function of $`\mathcal{D}`$, we can
write

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\cdot\mathbb{I}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}\right]`$ |  | (87) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j^{\star}_{i}\}\right]\right],`$ |  | (88) |

where $`\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]`$ is
sampled from the posterior distribution over $`\mathcal{I}`$ conditioned
on the dataset $`\mathcal{D}`$. Observe that conditioned on
$`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$, the posterior distribution over $`j_{i}^{\star}`$
under $`\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]`$ is
uniform over the set of indices $`j\in[M]`$ for which
$`(x_{i},y_{j})\notin\mathcal{D}`$, and this set has size at least
$`2`$. Hence,
$`\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j^{\star}_{i}\}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}`$,
and resuming from
[Eq. 88](#A8.E88 "In Lower bound under sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\{m_{i}\leq 2\Delta m/d\}\right]`$ |  | (89) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid m_{i}\leq 2\Delta m/d\right],`$ |  | (90) |

where the last inequality is from
[Eq. 86](#A8.E86 "In Lower bound under sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Finally, we can check that under the law $`\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}`$,
the probability of the event $`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$—conditioned on the
value $`m_{i}`$—is at least the probability that
$`(x_{i},y_{j^{\star}_{i}}),(x_{i},y_{j^{\prime}})\notin\mathcal{D}`$
for an arbitrary fixed index $`j^{\prime}\neq j^{\star}_{i}`$, which on
the event $`\{m_{i}\leq 2\Delta{}m/d\}`$ is at least

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{3}{M}\right)^{m_{i}}\geq\left(1-\frac{3}{M}\right)^{2\Delta m/d},`$ |  | (91) |

where we have used that $`\gamma=1/2`$. The value above is at least
$`\frac{1}{4}`$ whenever $`m\leq c\cdot{}dM/\Delta`$ for a sufficiently
small absolute constant $`c>0`$. For this value of $`m`$, we conclude
that
$`\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\geq\frac{1}{4}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid\{m_{i}\leq 2\Delta m/d\}\right]\geq\frac{1}{8}`$.
∎

###### Lower bound under adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle

In the adaptive framework, we let $`m_{i}`$ denote the (potentially
random) number of tuples $`(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x))`$
observed by the algorithm in which $`x=x_{i}`$. Note that unlike the
non-adaptive framework, the distribution over $`m_{i}`$ depends on the
underlying instance $`\mathcal{I}`$ with which the algorithm interacts.

To begin, from
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px3 "Preliminary technical results ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and Markov’s inequality, if $`\widehat{\pi}`$ satisfies the guarantee
$`\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}(x))>1/2]\geq 1-\epsilon`$,
then there exists a set of indices $`S_{\texttt{good}}\subset[d]`$ such
that¹⁴¹⁴ 14 We emphasize that the set $`S_{\texttt{good}}`$ is not a
random variable, and depends only on the algorithm itself.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle|S_{\texttt{good}}|\geq\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\quad\forall i\in S_{\texttt{good}},~\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\leq\frac{2\epsilon}{\Delta}.`$ |  | (92) |

We now appeal to the following lemma. {lemma} As long as $`M\geq{}6`$,
it holds that for all $`i\in[d]`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\geq{}\frac{1}{4e}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{m_{i}\leq{}M/3\}\right].`$ |  | (93) |

Combining
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px5 "Lower bound under adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
with
[Eq. 92](#A8.E92 "In Lower bound under adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
it follows that there exist absolute constant $`c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0`$
such that if $`\Delta=c_{1}\cdot{}\epsilon`$, then for all
$`i\in S_{\texttt{good}}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[m_{i}\geq{}c_{2}M\right]\geq{}c_{3}.`$ |  | (94) |

Thus, with this choice for $`\Delta`$, we have that
$`i\in S_{\texttt{good}}`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[m_{i}\right]\gtrsim M,`$ |  | (95) |

and we can lower bound the algorithm’s expected sample complexity by
summing over $`i\in S_{\texttt{good}}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[m\right]\geq\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\sum_{i\in S_{\texttt{good}}}m_{i}\right]\gtrsim|S_{\texttt{good}}|M\gtrsim dM.`$ |  | (96) |

The result now follows by tuning $`M\asymp 1+C\epsilon^{-1/p}`$ as in
the proof of the lower bound for non-adaptive sampling, which gives
$`\En[m]\gtrsim{}dM\asymp dC\epsilon^{-1/p}\asymp\epsilon^{-1/p}\log\Pi/\log(1+C\epsilon^{1/p})`$
and $`C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma,p}(\pi)\lesssim{}C`$ for all $`\pi\in\Pi`$.

Proof of
[Appendix H](#A8.SS0.SSS0.Px5 "Lower bound under adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Let $`i\in[d]`$ be fixed. Let
$`\mathcal{D}=\left\{(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x))\right\}`$
denote the dataset collected by the algorithm at termination, which has
$`\lvert\mathcal{D}\rvert=m`$. Let $`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$ denote the event
that, for prompt $`x=x_{i}`$, (i) there are at least two distinct
responses $`y_{j}`$ for which $`(x_{i},y_{j})\notin\mathcal{D}`$; and
(ii) there are no pairs $`(x_{i},y)\in\mathcal{D}`$ for which
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x_{i})>\frac{1}{M}`$. Since
$`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$ is a measurable function of $`\mathcal{D}`$, we can
write

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\cdot\mathbb{I}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}\right]`$ |  | (97) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j^{\star}_{i}\}\right]\right],`$ |  | (98) |

where $`\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]`$ is
sampled from the posterior distribution over $`\mathcal{I}`$ conditioned
on the dataset $`\mathcal{D}`$. Observe that conditioned on
$`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$, the posterior distribution over $`j_{i}^{\star}`$
under $`\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]`$ is
uniform over the set of indices $`j\in[M]`$ for which
$`(x_{i},y_{j})\notin\mathcal{D}`$, and this set has size at least
$`2`$. Hence,
$`\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{I}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{D}]}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j^{\star}_{i}\}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}`$,
and resuming from
[Eq. 98](#A8.E98 "In Lower bound under adaptive sample-and-evaluate oracle ‣ Appendix H Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\right]`$ |  | (99) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\cap\{m_{i}\leq M/3\}\right]`$ |  | (100) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\left[\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid m_{i}\leq M/3\right]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[m_{i}\leq{}M/3\right]\right].`$ |  | (101) |

The event $`\mathcal{E}_{i}`$ is a superset of the event
$`\mathcal{E}_{i,j^{\prime}}`$ that
$`(x_{i},y_{j^{\star}_{i}}),(x_{i},y_{j^{\prime}})\notin\mathcal{D}`$
for an arbitrary fixed index $`j^{\prime}\neq j^{\star}_{i}`$. Thus,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i}\mid m_{i}\leq M/3\right]\geq\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i,j^{\prime}}\mid m_{i}\leq M/3\right]`$ |  | (102) |

Moreover, we can realize the law of $`\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{I}}`$
considering an infinite tape, associated to index $`i`$, of i.i.d.
samples $`y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid x_{i})`$, and taking the
first $`m_{i}`$ elements on this tape to be the samples
$`(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x))\in\mathcal{D}`$ with
$`x=x_{i}`$ (see, e.g. [Simchowitz et al. (2017)](#bib.bib86) for an
argument of this form). On the event $`\{m_{i}\leq M/3\}`$, the
$`m_{i}`$ samples in
$`(x,y,\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x))\in\mathcal{D}`$ with
$`x=x_{i}`$ are a subset of the first $`M/3`$ samples from the
index-$`i`$ tape. Viewed in this way, we can lower bound the probability
of $`\mathcal{E}_{i,j}`$ by the probability of the event
$`\tilde{}\mathcal{E}_{i,j^{\prime}}`$ that the first $`M/3`$ $`y`$’s on
the index-$`i`$ tape contain neither $`j^{\star}_{i}`$, nor the
designated index $`j^{\prime}`$. As these first $`M/3`$ $`y`$’s are not
chosen adaptively, the probability of
$`\tilde{}\mathcal{E}_{i,j^{\prime}}`$ is at least

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{3}{M}\right)^{m_{i}}\geq\left(1-\frac{3}{M}\right)^{M/3}\geq\frac{1}{2e},`$ |  | (103) |

as long as $`M\geq{}6`$ and $`\gamma=1/2`$. We conclude that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{\widehat{j}_{i}\neq j_{i}^{\star}\}\right]\geq{}\frac{1}{4e}\En_{\mathcal{I}\sim\texttt{Unif}}\En^{\mathcal{I}}\left[\mathbb{I}\{m_{i}\leq{}M/3\}\right].`$ |  | (104) |

∎

### Appendix I Proofs from [Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism") and [Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

The following theorem is a generalization of
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
which allows for approximate maximizers in the sense of
[Section F.1](#A6.SS1 "F.1 Guarantees for Approximate Maximizers ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
{theorem} Let $`\rho,\delta\in(0,1)`$ be given, and suppose we set
$`N=N^{\star}\log(2\delta^{-1})`$ for a parameter
$`N^{\star}\in\mathbb{N}`$. Then for any $`n\in\mathbb{N}`$,
SFT-Sharpening ensures that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$, for
any $`\gamma\in(0,1)`$, the output model $`\widehat{\pi}`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-2\delta\right]\lesssim{}\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot{}\frac{\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})}{n}+\frac{C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}}{N^{\star}}.`$ |  | (105) |

In particular, given $`(\epsilon,\delta,\gamma)`$, by setting
$`n=C_{\ref{thm:bestofn}}\frac{\log\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\delta\epsilon}`$
and
$`N^{\star}=C_{\ref{thm:bestofn}}\frac{C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}}{\epsilon}`$
for a sufficiently large absolute constant $`C_{\ref{thm:bestofn}}>0`$,
we are guaranteed that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\leq\epsilon.`$ |  | (106) |

The total sample complexity is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle m=O\left(\frac{C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta\epsilon^{2}}\right).`$ |  | (107) |

Proof of
[Appendix I](#A9 "Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Under realizability of $`\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}`$
([Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
[Section F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Technical Tools ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
implies that the output of SFT-Sharpening satisfies, with probability at
least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\widehat{\pi}(\cdot\mid{}x),\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(\cdot\mid{}x)\right)\right]\leq\varepsilon_{\texttt{stat}}^{2}\vcentcolon={}\frac{2\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\rho)}{n}.`$ |  | (108) |

Henceforth we condition on the event that
[Eq. 108](#A9.E108 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
holds. Let

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}\vcentcolon={}\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}\mid N^{\star}\geq\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\right\}
``` |  | (109) |

denote the set of prompts for which $`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ places
sufficiently high mass on $`\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$. We can bound

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]`$ |  | (110) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad\leq{}\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta,x\in\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}\right]+\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[x\notin\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}\right].`$ |  | (111) |

To bound the first term in
[Eq. 111](#A9.E111 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
note that if $`x\in\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}`$, then
$`\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\geq{}1-\delta/2`$.
Indeed, observe that
$`y\sim\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(\cdot\mid{}x)\notin\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$
if and only if $`y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)`$ have
$`y_{i}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)`$ for all $`i`$, which happens
with probability
$`(1-\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x))^{N}\leq(1-1/N^{\star})^{N}\leq\delta/2`$
since $`x\in\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}`$. It follows that for any such
$`x`$, we can lower bound (using the data processing inequality)

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\widehat{\pi}(\cdot\mid{}x),\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(\cdot\mid{}x)\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\left(\sqrt{1-\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}-\sqrt{1-\pi^{\texttt{BoN}}_{N}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\right)^{2}`$ |  | (112) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\gtrsim{}\delta\cdot\mathbb{I}\left\{\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right\}.`$ |  | (113) |

By
[Eqs. 108](#A9.E108 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and [113](#A9.E113 "Equation 113 ‣ Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
it follows that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta,x\in\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}\right]\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon_{\texttt{stat}}^{2}}{\delta}.
``` |  | (114) |

For the second term in
[Eq. 111](#A9.E111 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
we bound

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[x\notin\mathcal{X}_{\texttt{good}}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[N^{\star}<\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\right]`$ |  | (115) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{N^{\star}\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}>1\right]`$ |  | (116) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\frac{1}{N^{\star}}\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\right]`$ |  | (117) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\frac{C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}}{N^{\star}}`$ |  | (118) |

via Markov’s inequality and the definition of
$`C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}`$. Substituting both bounds into
[Eq. 111](#A9.E111 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
completes the proof. ∎

Proof of
[Appendix D](#A4.SS0.SSS0.Px2 "Theoretical guarantee ‣ Appendix D Guarantees for SFT-Sharpening with Adaptive Sampling ‣ Part I Additional Discussion and Results ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
The proof begins similarly to
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Analysis of SFT-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
By realizability of $`\pi_{N_{\mu}}`$,
[Section F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Technical Tools ‣ Appendix F Preliminaries ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
implies that the output of SFT-Sharpening satisfies, with probability at
least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu}\left[D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\widehat{\pi}(\cdot\mid{}x),\pi_{N_{\mu}}(\cdot\mid{}x)\right)\right]\leq\varepsilon_{\texttt{stat}}^{2}\vcentcolon={}\frac{2\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\rho)}{n}.`$ |  | (119) |

Condition on the event that this guarantee holds. We invoke the
following lemma, proven in the sequel. {lemma} Let $`P`$ be a
distribution on a discrete space $`\mathcal{Y}`$. Let
$`\bm{y}^{\star}=\argmax_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P(y)`$ and let
$`P^{\star}:=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}P(y)`$. Let
$`y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\sim{}P`$, and for any stopping time $`\tau`$,
define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{\tau}\in\argmax\left\{P(y):y\in\left\{y_{1},\dots,y_{\tau}\right\}\right\}.`$ |  | (120) |

Next, for a parameter $`\mu>0`$, define the stopping time

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle N_{\mu}:=\inf\left\{k:\frac{1}{\max_{1\leq i\leq k}P(y_{i})}\leq k/\mu\right\}.`$ |  | (121) |

Then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En[N_{\mu}]\leq\frac{\mu+(1/|\bm{y}^{\star}|)}{P^{\star}}.`$ |  | (122) |

In addition, for any stopping time $`\tau\geq N_{\mu}`$ (including
$`\tau=N_{\mu}`$ itself), we have
$`\mathbb{P}[\widehat{y}_{\tau}\notin\bm{y}^{\star}]\leq e^{-\lvert\bm{y}^{\star}\rvert\mu}`$.
This lemma, with our choice of $`\mu`$, ensures that *for all
$`x\in\mathcal{X}`$*,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\pi_{N_{\mu}}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid x)\geq 1-e^{-\mu}=1-\delta/2.`$ |  | (123) |

Following the reasoning in
[Eq. 113](#A9.E113 "In Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
this implies that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathsf{H}}\left(\widehat{\pi}(\cdot\mid{}x),\pi_{N_{\mu}}(\cdot\mid{}x)\right)\gtrsim{}\delta\cdot\mathbb{I}\left\{\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right\},`$ |  | (124) |

so that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon_{\texttt{stat}}^{2}}{\delta}`$ |  | (125) |

as desired.

To bound the expected sample complexity, we observe that

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En[m]=n\cdot\En[N_{\mu}(x)]\overset{(i)}{\leq}\En\left[\frac{1+\mu}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}(x)\mid x)}\right]=(1+\mu)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{C}_{\texttt{cov}},`$ |  | (126) |

where inequality $`(i)`$ invokes
[Appendix I](#A9 "Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
once more. ∎

Proof of
[Appendix I](#A9 "Appendix I Proofs from and ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Define $`N^{\star}:=\mu/P^{\star}`$. To bound the tails of $`N_{\mu}`$,
define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tau=\inf\{k\mid{}k\geq N^{\star}\text{ and }\bm{y}^{\star}\cap\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}\neq\varnothing\}.`$ |  | (127) |

It follows from the definition that $`N_{\mu}\leq\tau`$, since for any
$`k\geq{}N^{\star}`$, if there exists $`i\leq{}k`$ such that
$`y_{i}\in\bm{y}^{\star}`$, then

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\frac{1}{P(y_{i})}=\frac{1}{P^{\star}}=\frac{N^{\star}}{\mu}\leq\frac{k}{\mu}.`$ |  | (128) |

Thus, for $`k\geq N^{\star}`$, we can bound

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[N_{\mu}>k]\leq\mathbb{P}[\tau>k]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\cap\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}=\varnothing]\leq(1-|\bm{y}^{\star}|P^{\star})^{k},`$ |  | (129) |

and consequently

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En[N_{\mu}]\leq\En[\tau]`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\En[\tau\mathbb{I}\{\tau\leq N^{\star}\}]+\En[\tau\mathbb{I}\{\tau>N^{\star}\}]`$ |  | (130) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq N^{\star}+\sum_{k>N^{\star}}(1-|\bm{y}^{\star}|P^{\star})^{k}`$ |  | (131) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq N^{\star}+\frac{1}{|\bm{y}^{\star}|P(y^{\star})}=\frac{\mu+1/|\bm{y}^{\star}|}{P(y^{\star})}.`$ |  | (132) |

To prove correctness, observe that $`N_{\mu}\geq N^{\star}`$, because
for all $`y\in\mathcal{Y}`$, $`\frac{1}{P(y)}\geq N^{\star}/\mu`$.
Hence, any stopping time $`\tau\geq N_{\mu}`$ also satisfies
$`\tau\geq N^{\star}`$, and moreover has
$`\widehat{y}_{\tau}\in\bm{y}^{\star}`$ whenever
$`\bm{y}^{\star}\cap\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{\tau}\}\neq\varnothing`$.
This fails to occur with probability no more than

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{|\bm{y}^{\star}|}{P^{\star}}\right)^{N^{\star}}=\left(1-\frac{|\bm{y}^{\star}|}{P^{\star}}\right)^{\mu/P^{\star}}\leq e^{-|\bm{y}^{\star}|\mu}.`$ |  | (133) |

∎

### Appendix J Proofs from [Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

#### J.1 Proof of [Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px2 "Main result ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

We state and prove a generalized version of
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px2 "Main result ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
In the assumptions below, we fix a parameter $`\gamma\in[0,1)`$; the
setting $`\gamma=0`$ corresponds to
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px2 "Main result ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

{assumption}

\[Coverage\] All $`\pi\in\Pi`$ satisfy
$`\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\leq C_{\texttt{conc}}`$ for a parameter
$`C_{\texttt{conc}}\geq(1-\gamma)^{-1}C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}`$, and
$`\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta}\leq C_{\texttt{loss}}`$
for a parameter $`C_{\texttt{loss}}\geq\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert`$. By
[Section J.1.1](#A10.SS1.SSS1 "J.1.1 Technical lemmas ‣ J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism"),
[Section J.1](#A10.SS1 "J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
is consistent with the assumption that $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\in\Pi`$.

{assumption}

\[Margin\] For all $`x\in\mathrm{supp}(\mu)`$, the initial model
$`\pi_{\texttt{base}}`$ satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\geq{}(1+\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}})\cdot\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\quad\forall{}y\not\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)
``` |  | (134) |

for a parameter $`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}>0`$.

{theorem}

Assume that $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}\in\Pi`$
([Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")),
and that
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
and
[Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
hold with respect to some $`\gamma\in[0,1)`$, with parameters
$`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$, $`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$, and
$`\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}>0`$. For any $`\delta,\rho\in(0,1)`$, the DPO
algorithm in
[Eq. 7](#S2.E7 "In 2.2 Self-Improvement through RLHF: RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 2 Sharpening Algorithms for Self-Improvement ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")
ensures that with probability at least $`1-\rho`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x\sim\mu}\left[\widehat{\pi}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\leq{}1-\delta\right]\lesssim{}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mathsf{margin}}\delta}\cdot{}\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{C_{\texttt{conc}}\log^{3}(C_{\texttt{loss}}\lvert\Pi\rvert\rho^{-1})}{n}}+\beta\log(C_{\texttt{conc}})+\gamma\right)`$ |  | (135) |

where $`\widetilde{O}\left(\cdot\right)`$ hides factors logarithmic in
$`n`$ and $`C_{\texttt{conc}}`$ and doubly logarithmic in $`\Pi`$,
$`C_{\texttt{loss}}`$, and $`\rho^{-1}`$.

We first state and prove some supporting technical lemmas, then proceed
to the proof of
[Section J.1](#A10.SS1 "J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

##### J.1.1 Technical lemmas

The following result is a generalization of
[Section 4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1.Px1 "Assumptions ‣ 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").

{lemma}

For all $`\gamma\in(0,1)`$, the model $`\pi^{\star}_{\beta}`$ satisfies
$`\mathcal{C}_{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}\leq(1-\gamma)^{-1}C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}`$
and
$`\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}};\beta}\leq{}\left\lvert\mathcal{Y}\right\rvert`$.

Proof of
[Section J.1.1](#A10.SS1.SSS1 "J.1.1 Technical lemmas ‣ J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
For any fixed $`x\in\mathcal{X}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{y\sim\pi^{\star}_{\beta}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[\frac{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}(y\mid x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid x)}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\En_{y\sim\pi^{\star}_{\beta}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right]\cdot{}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{-1}`$ |  | (136) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{\beta^{-1}}(y\mid{}x)\cdot{}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{-1}`$ |  | (137) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}(1-\gamma)^{-1}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{\beta^{-1}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)\cdot{}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{-1}`$ |  | (138) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)^{-1}\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\cdot{}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{-1}`$ |  | (139) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)^{-1}\frac{\sum_{y\in\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}\cdot{}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{-1}`$ |  | (140) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq(1-\gamma)^{-1}\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\bm{y}^{\star}_{\gamma}(x)\mid{}x)}.`$ |  | (141) |

It follows that
$`\mathcal{C}_{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}\leq(1-\gamma)^{-1}C_{\texttt{cov},\gamma}`$
as claimed.

For the second result, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi^{\star}_{\beta}}};\beta}=\En_{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\cdot\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\pi_{\texttt{base}}^{1+\beta^{-1}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)\right)^{\beta}\right]\leq{}\En_{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right]=\lvert\mathcal{Y}\rvert.`$ |  | (142) |

∎

The next lemmas provide bounds on the tails of the self-rewards used in
the algorithm.

{lemma}

Suppose $`\beta\in[0,1]`$. For any model $`\pi`$, with probability at
least $`1-\delta`$ over the draw of $`x\sim\mu`$,
$`y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid x)`$, we have that for
all $`s>0`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left\lvert\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right\rvert>\log(2\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta})+s\right]\leq\exp\left(-s\right).`$ |  | (143) |

Proof of
[Section J.1.1](#A10.SS1.SSS1 "J.1.1 Technical lemmas ‣ J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
X\vcentcolon=\left\lvert\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right\rvert.
``` |  | (144) |

By the Chernoff method, we have that with probability at least
$`1-\delta`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle X`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\log(\En\left[\exp(X)\right])+\log(\delta^{-1})`$ |  | (145) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left(\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\exp\left(\left\lvert\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right\rvert\right)\right]\right)+\log(\delta^{-1})`$ |  | (146) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\log\Bigg(\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\exp\left(\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right)\right]`$ |  | (147) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\qquad+\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\exp\left(\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)\right)\right]\Bigg)+\log(\delta^{-1})`$ |  | (148) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left(2\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\exp\left(\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right)\right]\right)+\log(\delta^{-1})`$ |  | (149) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\log\left(\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\cdot\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)^{\beta}\right]\right)+\log(2\delta^{-1}).`$ |  | (150) |

As long as $`\beta\leq{}1`$, by Jensen’s inequality, we can bound

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\cdot\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)^{\beta}\right]`$ |  | (151) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}\En_{x\sim\mu,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\left(\En_{y\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right]\cdot\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)^{\beta}\right]`$ |  | (152) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(x)}\left[\left(\frac{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)^{\beta}\right]`$ |  | (153) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta},`$ |  | (154) |

which proves the result. ∎

{lemma}

Let $`\beta\in[0,1]`$. For all models $`\pi`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu,y,y^{\prime}\sim\pi_{\texttt{base}}(\cdot\mid x)}\left[\left\lvert\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right\rvert^{4}\right]\leq{}O(\log^{4}(\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta})+1).`$ |  | (155) |

Proof of
[Section J.1.1](#A10.SS1.SSS1 "J.1.1 Technical lemmas ‣ J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
X\vcentcolon=\left\lvert\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)}\right)-\beta\log\left(\frac{\pi(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}{\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y^{\prime}\mid{}x)}\right)\right\rvert.
``` |  | (156) |

Set
$`k=\log(2\mathcal{C}_{\nicefrac{{\pi_{\texttt{base}}}}{{\pi}};\beta})`$.
We can bound

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\En\left[X^{4}\right]`$ | $`\displaystyle=\En\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}\left\{X^{4}>t\right\}dt\right]`$ |  | (157) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=4\En\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}\left\{X>t\right\}t^{3}dt\right]`$ |  | (158) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[X>t\right]t^{3}dt`$ |  | (159) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}k^{4}+4\int_{k}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[X>t\right]t^{3}dt`$ |  | (160) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\leq{}k^{4}+4\int_{k}^{\infty}e^{k-t}t^{3}dt`$ |  | (161) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=k^{4}+4(k^{3}+3k^{2}+6k+6)`$ |  | (162) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=O(k^{4}+1),`$ |  | (163) |

where the third-to-last line uses
[Section J.1.1](#A10.SS1.SSS1 "J.1.1 Technical lemmas ‣ J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
∎

##### J.1.2 Proof of [Section J.1](#A10.SS1 "J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism")

Proof of
[Section J.1](#A10.SS1 "J.1 Proof of ‣ Appendix J Proofs from ‣ Part II Proofs ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
For any model $`\pi\in\Pi`$, define
$`J(\pi)\vcentcolon={}\En_{\pi}\left[\log\pi_{\texttt{base}}(y\mid{}x)\right]`$.
Let $`\widehat{\pi}\in\Pi`$ denote the model returned by the DPO
algorithm in
[Eq. 21](#S4.E21 "In 4.2.1 Guarantees for RLHF-Sharpening with Direct Preference Optimization ‣ 4.2 Analysis of RLHF-Sharpening ‣ 4 Analysis of Sharpening Algorithms ‣ Self-Improvement in Language Models:The Sharpening Mechanism").
Let $`\En_{\pi,\pi^{\prime}}\left[\cdot\right]`$ denote shorthand for
$`\En_{x\sim\mu,y\sim\pi(x),y^{\prime}\sim\pi^{\prime}(x)}[\cdot]`$, and
for any $`r:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}`$ define
$`\Delta^{r}(x,y,y^{\prime}):=r(x,y)-r(x,y^{\prime})`$. Define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
````
